# Arnold Houbraken: Study Notes

**Site:** Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660, Dordrecht - 14 October 1719, Amsterdam), Dutch painter, engraver, etcher, mezzotinter and art writer; the father who wrote the book his son engraved. He was the son of a Dordrecht cloth-darner, apprenticed at nine to a thread-twister, and learned painting from Samuel van Hoogstraten -- four years he called "the foundation of everything I know about art" (Q1). He married Sara Sasbout in 1685, fathered ten children, and moved to Amsterdam c. 1709 at the invitation of the collector Jonas Witsen. In 1712 he published the deist *Philalethes Brieven*, was censured by the Amsterdam Church Council in 1713 and fled to London for nine months. His *De groote schouburgh der Nederlantsche konstschilders en schilderessen* (1718-1721) names or discusses 1,207 artists, was printed for himself by subscription at six guilders, and was finished after his death by his widow and children; it remains the single most important source of information about the great Dutch painters of the seventeenth century. He died 14 October 1719 of a wasting illness, aged 59. Horn's 2023 archival work finds he was baptised as an adult Mennonite, as Aert, on 30 June 1680 (see C574) -- the name Arnold is the professional name, not the baptismal one.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Jacobus Houbraken study: the son engraved his father's book, four hundred faces in a standard oval, each one annotated with the painting it came from and the collection that held it; now meet the father who wrote the book the son engraved -- the painter-biographer who turned three volumes of lives into a theatre, and whose own life is the first scene in it. The doorway is the father whose book the son engraved.

One-hundred-and-sixty-seventh doorway check in a row. D1 "Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 - 14 October 1719)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (EN Wikipedia read end to end by legs 1, 2 and 3, 171 lines each; DE/NL/FR Wikipedia agree; the DA Wikipedia infobox's 10 February 1719 is the lone error, against its own article text -- leg 1 C11). D4 "wrote the *Groote Schouburgh* Jacobus illustrated" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (vols. I-III 1718, 1719, 1721 -- leg 1 F34). ## The life

Arnold Houbraken was born 28 March 1660 in Dordrecht, the son of Jan Jansz Houbraken (died 1676), a 'lakenstopper' or cloth darner, and Geertruyt Aertsdr (died 1679), who is not once mentioned in his extensive written oeuvre (leg 1 F1-F3). He was born into a Mennonite family in a small, closed Dordrecht community -- probably fewer than 1,000 individuals, about 250 of them baptised members -- which rejected predestination and infant baptism; he eventually gave up the infant-baptism issue, and all his children were duly baptised as infants (leg 1 F4). Horn's archival finding is that he was himself baptised as an adult, as Aert, on 30 June 1680, after both parents had died (leg 1 F2-F3; see C574). He had almost no formal education beyond a modest amount of the three R's, plus Sunday school and advanced religious instruction in his faith (leg 1 F5).

At nine he was apprenticed for two years to Johannes de Haan (de Haen, 1650-1730), a merchant in twine, a former student of Nicolaes Maes, who encouraged his interest in drawing and introduced him to engraving -- de Haen would have been only nineteen at the time, Horn's own observation (leg 1 F6). After two years with de Haan he studied art with Willem van Drielenburch (c. 1625-after 1677), a landscape painter from Utrecht, during the rampjaar 1672; then nine months with Jacobus Levecq (1634-1675), a former Rembrandt student and portraitist; and finally four years (1674-1678) with Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-1678) (leg 1 F7). His first book illustration appeared in 1678, while still van Hoogstraten's pupil: the etching "About the Shadows of the Sun, and Her Striations", 70 x 121 mm, in van Hoogstraten's Inleyding (1678), p. 269 -- an accomplished etching with closely observed light effects. Had he been given all the Inleyding illustrations, as he had hoped, it would have jump-started his career as inventor by four years (leg 1 F8). He became a member of the Dordrecht Guild of St Luke in 1678 (leg 1 F9). He called his years with van Hoogstraten "the foundation of everything I know about art" (Q1); van Hoogstraten entrusted him with his second, unpublished theoretical treatise, and Houbraken later planned to edit and publish his master's Onzigtbare Waereld (Invisible World) after completing the Schouburgh (leg 1 F10). The relationship was ambivalent and is recorded at length in the Schouburgh: he accused van Hoogstraten of mindless and indiscriminate ambition, of courting the ignorant in his last years with things he had denounced in his book, and of being a better theoretician than artist (leg 1 F11).

In 1685 he married Sara Sasbout (also given as Sara Sasbout Souburg, 1662-1729; anticstore spells her "Sara Souborg"), daughter of the physician Jacob Sasbout Souburg (leg 1 F12; see leg 1 C4 on the name variants). He had ten children; three are named in the sources read: Antonina (born 30 May 1686, baptised 31 May 1686; buried Amsterdam 12 December 1736), draughtswoman and illustrator, married the topographical draughtsman Jacobus Stellingwerff (1667-1727); Christina (c. 1695-1760s), painter and draughtswoman, married the painter Anthoni Elliger (1701-1781); Jacobus (baptised Dordrecht 17 December 1698; buried Amsterdam 18 November 1780), engraver (leg 1 F13; see C569 on Antonina's dates). Antonina is known today for her embellishment of cityscapes and buildings with animals and people; Christina was also an artist; their daughter Christina Maria became a draughtswoman (leg 1 F14). In Dordrecht he lived in a house named "Maastricht" on the Varkenmarkt (leg 1 F15, single source).

He settled as an independent artist in Dordrecht after his training; in Dordrecht he mostly painted portraits, while in Amsterdam's cultural climate he was able to concentrate on mythological subjects in a refined academic manner (leg 1 F16). Around 1709 he moved with his family from Dordrecht to Amsterdam, at the invitation of / prompted by the Amsterdam regent and art collector Jonas (Johan) Witsen, a wealthy Maecenas (leg 1 F17; see C576). Geerts.com suggests he probably left Dordrecht because he could develop himself too little there as a history painter (leg 1 F18, weak source, flagged). A domestic scandal in Dordrecht likely encouraged the move; in Amsterdam he became embroiled in a bitter dispute with his friend Jan van Hoogstraten over the production of De Kruisheld, leading to Lyris, a poem that made Jan van Hoogstraten and his wife look ridiculous (leg 1 F24).

Raised a Mennonite, he married as a Calvinist in 1685 but had evolved into a deist by the time he published his Philalethes Brieven in 1712. The next year (1713) the Church Council of Amsterdam censured his "abominable" ideas, forcing him to flee to London for nine months. Though he stressed the importance of Christ's passion, he dismissed or ignored all other Christian dogma. His belief in a hands-off God whose wonderful but remote plan can be glimpsed only through the study of nature underlies the biographies, predilections and theory of his Groote schouburgh (leg 1 F20). By 20 July 1713 a delegation had been to see Houbraken to demand he explain himself (leg 1 F21). In London in 1713 he visited the house of Philip Wharton, where he saw 32 portraits by Van Dyck (leg 1 F22, single source). He also made portrait drawings in London, ten of which were engraved and issued clandestinely by Pieter van Gunst, with a few more probably published posthumously (leg 1 F22). No 1680 trip to England is established: it is claimed by anticstore only, and no other source in this run mentions it (leg 1 F26/C6; see C570).

His books came thick and fast. Tooneel van sinnebeelden (Theatre of Emblems), 1700, his first book, oriented on Cesare Ripa's Iconologia (leg 1 F27). Inhoud van 't Sieraad der Afbeelding (Contents of the Ornament of Depiction), 1712, in Des menschen begin, midden en einde -- an emblem book meant as a guide of possible painting themes, his first attempt at an instructive manual for artists (leg 1 F28). Stichtelyke zinnebeelden gepast op deugden en ondeugden in LVII tafereellen vertoont (Edifying Emblems, 57 plates), written 1714 with Gesina Brit, who wrote ten-line poems for Houbraken's prints; published posthumously in 1723 (leg 1 F29; see leg 1 C7). Philalethes Brieven (Letters of Philalethes), 1712 -- the deist letters that caused the 1713 censure (leg 1 F30). He also planned an Ovid commentary and a numismatic reference work, announced in volumes I and II of the Schouburgh; Horn calls these "probably no more than wishful thinking" (leg 1 F32).

The Groote schouburgh der Nederlantsche konstschilders en schilderessen (The Great Theatre of Dutch Painters and Paintresses), three volumes: vol. I 1718, vol. II 1719, vol. III posthumously 1721, published by his widow Sara and children. Self-published: "Gedrukt voor den autheur, daar de zelve ook te bekomen zyn" (printed for the author, obtainable from him); vol. III "Gedrukt voor de Weduwe des Autheurs" (leg 1 F34). Conceived as a sequel/continuation of Karel van Mander's Schilder-boeck (1604). Vol. I was simply meant as an addendum to van Mander, listing about 200 artists born between 1466 (starting with Erasmus) and 1613 (ending with Jacques van Artois) omitted by van Mander or whose lives extended beyond 1604. Its popularity led immediately to vol. II and plans to continue to Houbraken's own time (leg 1 F35). Houbraken names or discusses 1,207 artists, of whom 1,015 are Dutch or Flemish painters and glass painters; the remaining 192 are engravers, etchers, gold and silver smiths, sculptors and architects, as well as foreign artists. The artists are brought onstage beginning with Desiderius Erasmus (c. 1467/9-1536), "who is also to have painted", and ending with Adriaen van den Werff (1659-1722), the most successful painter of his time (leg 1 F36). The book is enriched by forty-three plates with double or triple portraits of artists drawn by Houbraken and engraved by his son Jacob, plus seven other plates of varied subject matter (Horn 2023; the Foreword says "all the 45 original illustrations" are included -- see leg 1 C8 on the counts). The portraits were engraved by Jacobus; Swillens specified only one of Houbraken's own etchings for the Schouburgh, "no doubt because the many portraits were engraved by his son Jacob" (leg 1 F37). Houbraken commenced the Groote schouburgh in 1715 in the sense of collecting material, taking notes and drawing portrait illustrations in 1715-1716, but only started composing and writing the actual book in 1717 -- so he wrote three substantial volumes within three years, by quill, without typewriter or photocopier (leg 1 F38). His writing method: for most of his life he assembled antiquarian information; only in the last few years before the Schouburgh did he begin correspondence with ancestors and students of deceased painters to learn about their background, training, travel and patrons, along with their financial successes or failures and most important achievements (leg 1 F39). Sources he used: van Mander, Sandrart's Teutsche Akademie (1668), Cornelis de Bie's Het Gulden Cabinet (1662), Felibien, Roger de Piles, Florent le Comte, his teacher van Hoogstraten's Inleyding (1678), Jan Meyssens' Image de divers hommes (1649, after van Dyck's Iconography), local history books from various Dutch cities, contacts via his professional network (mostly members of the Guild of St. Luke), a competency list drawn up by Vincent van der Vinne before his death in 1702, and the Bentvueghels list from a poem. He mentions his sources in the first chapter of vol. I (leg 1 F40). He used a system in which names in UPPERCASE denoted greater prominence, lowercase lesser recognition. The index of vol. I is believed to have been incomplete at publication; a later edition (1756) contains an improved index (leg 1 F41). He gave little thought to evaluating his authorities: he simply used the most recent source even if it was obviously copied from an earlier one, and sometimes opted for a later but inferior source (e.g. Louis Moreri on Van Dyck). Hofstede de Groot (1893) documented numerous errors of judgment and three detailed pages of Fluchtigkeitsfehler (errors brought on by haste) (leg 1 F42). He did not wait until a volume was complete before handing it to the printers: material of a later date appears out of chronological order because it was already with the printers; when he discovered he had been seriously misinformed he corrected his material even if it meant returning to an earlier volume (e.g. the deaths of Pieter van Laer and Johannes Torrentius; a portrait by Juriaen van Streek of Emanuel de Witte's wife, first wrongly given to de Witte himself) (leg 1 F43). He repeatedly laments that potential witnesses have died or that the living have not been forthcoming with biographical data or a portrait; he begs his readers for supplemental information (e.g. for Bartholomeus Breenbergh, Gabriel Metsu, Gerard ter Borch, Gerard Pietersz van Zijl, "all painters worthy of a major role in our theatre"). He admits he was halfway through his "work with the pen" before he discovered Erasmus was active as an artist (leg 1 F44). The RKD Houbraken Translated project identified 294 collectors mentioned in the book and 521 works named by Houbraken, all illustrated and linked to RKDimages (leg 1 F45). Subscription price was six guilders -- three to be paid at once and three more upon delivery -- set too low to meet production costs for such a fine work, so "our honest artist always shed behind the net, ploughed the parched beach and hunted where there was naught to be caught" (van Gool 1750, p. 138, quoted in Horn 2023, p. 219). The money saved by having his son Jacob engrave the many portraits could not make enough difference. Although there was a ready market, the book yielded little profit (leg 1 F46). Francois Fagel (1659-1740) declined a dedication of the first volume; Houbraken then turned to Johan van Schuylenburch (1675-1735), who accepted the handsomely bound copy but received the second volume "with a slight smile and scant thanks". Houbraken had taken along a picture as a gift; Schuylenburg did not want it (leg 1 F47). In April 1718 an anonymous author published a thoroughly positive review of vol. I in the Amsterdam trade periodical Maendelyke uittreksels, of boekzael der geleerde waerelt (Monthly Abstracts, or Book Room of the Learned World; 96 issues, 1716-1811), commending Houbraken for rescuing artists from oblivion. The review quotes Houbraken's own verse and tales, including Rembrandt throwing a libidinous young couple out into the street after catching them acting out Adam and Eve (leg 1 F48). Second edition: The Hague, 1753, in somewhat modified form; a facsimile was published in 1976 by B. M. Israel BV, Amsterdam (leg 1 F49). The Schouburgh is part of the Basic Library of the dbnl (Database of Dutch Literature), among the 1,000 most important works in Dutch literature from the Middle Ages to today (leg 1 F50). The complete English translation, Houbraken Translated (RKD Studies, 2023, assembled by Hendrik J. Horn and Rieke van Leeuwen), gives the full text with the original Dutch scanned from the RKD rare-books collection, hyperlinked names, and the 45 original illustrations (leg 1 F51).

As a painter he was a history painter of the learned kind. Hofstede de Groot's 1893 catalogue lists over 130 works: ten Old Testament subjects, twenty-three New Testament ones, thirty-two mythologies and profane histories, five allegories, thirty-three genre paintings, twenty-two portraits, five landscapes and three unidentified subjects (leg 1 F52). His mythological and religious paintings are mostly on medium-size canvases with elaborate and complicated compositions and large numbers of small figures, in a Northern tradition starting with Cornelis van Poelenburch and continued by Nicolaes Knupfer, Aert Jansz Marienhof, Jan Steen, Willem van Mieris and Nicolaas Verkolje. Attributions are not always secure: in one case Bok was unsure whether a history painting was by Houbraken or Verkolje (leg 1 F66). Alan Chong called him "a history painter of considerable finesse and erudition, whose treatment of rarified subjects is especially noteworthy" (leg 1 F67). He was first and foremost an artist whose overall production was dominated by his inventions for the book trade, whether autograph etchings or preparatory drawings for the engravings of others (leg 1 F68). Piet Swillens compiled the most recent and complete inventory: 24 books with one or more illustrations by Houbraken (all dating from 1678 or later), adding up to more than 425 illustrations; Horn corrects Swillens (omitted eight works, included one incorrect attribution) to a total of 31 books illustrated, with about 150 of the illustrations dating from his Dordrecht years (leg 1 F69). Hollstein catalogued a rich selection of his etchings, including independent biblical and mythological subjects such as The Return of the Prodigal Son and The Three Daughters of Cecrops Finding the Little Erichthonius in a Basket with a Serpent (large undated etching, 367 x 275 mm, Rijksprentenkabinet, probably based on Italian models), plus erotic prints directly inspired by engravings from Annibale Carracci's Lascivie series of c. 1590-1595 (leg 1 F70). Hollstein also included three of Houbraken's mezzotints, "an acquired taste": their ability to approach the tonal values of a painting, and their peculiar unctuous and swarthy effect, is at once apparent in A Nocturnal Landscape with a Shepherd, Nymph and Child (leg 1 F71). His unsigned title print for the anonymously published Laetsten Duyvels-Dreck (1687) shows a pope riding on the back of a pig feeding his own faeces to his mount while a gullible crowd cheers -- rabid anti-Catholic propaganda; the masterful rendering of the faces of the background spectators "heralds his lasting concern with convincing physiognomy" (leg 1 F72).

His registered pupils were Matthijs Balen (1684-1766), Johan Graham, and his son Jacobus (leg 1 F75; Balen's dates are parent-verified in this run, see C568). FR Wikipedia lists Jan Claesz Rietschoof as a pupil -- not in EN Wikipedia's RKD-based list; single source, flagged (leg 1 F76/C10).

He died 14 October 1719 in Amsterdam of what Johan van Gool called "a wasting illness"; van Gool blamed the major shortcomings of vol. III on the biographer's failing health, though it is not clear when the illness set in (leg 1 F77). When he died, his son Jacob assisted his mother with the last proofs of the manuscript before publishing vol. III (1721). His daughter Antonina and his wife also helped patch things up for publication; their own opinions may have slipped into the finished work (leg 1 F78). The third volume's opening "message for the reader" offers a corrected index to the location of the plates, and its closing summary is shorter and simply numbered end to end, unlike vols. I-II -- evidence that Sara Houbraken (or an anonymous helper) lacked the insight to imitate Houbraken's thematic approach when completing his work (leg 1 F79). His widow sold thirty paintings by Houbraken and colleagues on 17 July 1720 to help fund the third volume -- suggesting Houbraken dealt in pictures on occasion (leg 1 F80).

His books sold quite well throughout the 18th century. Jacob Campo Weyerman published his updated version (1729-1747) in serial form, complete set 1769. Johan van Gool published the first sequel, Nieuwe schouburg (1750-51) (leg 1 F81). In the 19th century Houbraken fell out of favour with art historians, especially when his sketches were found wanting, incorrect, or even slanderous. He was very careful to check and double-check his sources, and today many of his personal judgements still stand up to modern scrutiny (leg 1 F82). The first modern art historian to publish an update of his work was Adriaan van der Willigen, in 1866 (leg 1 F83). Cornelis Hofstede de Groot's Arnold Houbraken und seine 'Groote Schouburgh' kritisch beleuchtet (1893) is the seminal critical study; Alfred von Wurzbach's German translation appeared 1880 (Vienna); P. T. A. Swillens reprinted vols. I-II in 1943-44 (Maastricht: Leiter-Nypels) and vol. III in 1953 (leg 1 F84). Jan Emmens (1924-1971) argued Houbraken's classicism was a "compilatory, thoughtless and narrowminded" version of Gerard de Lairesse and the French, and that his Philalethes brieven showed his talent for theorising was "about as small as his need for it was great". Horn argues Emmens completely overlooked the critical religious component of Houbraken's thought (leg 1 F85). His Rembrandt biography in vol. I (1718) crystallised Rembrandt criticism for many later 18th- and 19th-century authors, who wrote about Rembrandt on the basis of facts, ideas and anecdotes read in Houbraken instead of studying the oeuvre (leg 1 F86). Houbraken's engravings of the artists are in some cases the only surviving portraits of these people (leg 1 F87). Public collections holding his work: Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam; Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam; Dordrechts Museum; Stadel, Frankfurt (Rudolf van Loen portrait); Rijksprentenkabinet (etchings/mezzotints) (leg 1 F88; no Boijmans object was identified in this run -- leg 2 F13). His Schouburgh was the first extensive study of the lives and works of 17th-century Netherlandish painters (Slive). It remains the single most important source of information about the great Dutch painters of the 17th century (Britannica), though Britannica calls him "a competent if rather uninspired academic painter" (leg 1 F89/Q10).

He was a deistic Stoic (neo-Stoic): his emblem book was encouraged by his friend Jacob Zeeus; his beliefs underlie the Schouburgh's biographies, predilections and theory. As a deist he believed lives end with "the dark night of death" and the only thing he could do to save his subjects from eternal darkness was to record their names and works for posterity (leg 1 F90). A 2021 VU paper (N. Schroeder, Church History and Religious Culture 101 (2021), 324-356) treats him as an unconventional Christian and examines his representation of religious minority groups -- the spiritualist David Joris and the Mennonite martyr Jan Woutersz van Cuyck among them -- and his Flemish Mennonite milieu in Dordrecht (1660-c. 1685) (leg 1 F91, snippet only). Houbraken includes an account of the Mennonite martyr Jan Woutersz van Cuyck, prefaced by a fervent plea for tolerance and humanity in the face of religious fanaticism (leg 1 F92). His theoretical digressions, which he believed of great importance, are loaded with learned antiquarian information intended for young history painters; his convictions are often "virtually encoded" (leg 1 F93).

The Schouburgh's biases, as documented: he follows the hierarchy of genres and undervalues landscapists, marine artists and still-life painters. Family dynasties receive extra space, more for the founder than any other member (he considered himself founder of his own dynasty). He preferred artists skilled in drafting and engraving (Rembrandt, the Visschers). He respected all who travelled to Rome. He emphasised flattering patrons; artists who fell on bad times for failing to flatter a patron are treated as martyrs. Mennonites are over-represented, Catholics under-represented (leg 1 F94). Notorious omissions: Jan Vermeer is mentioned once in passing; also briefly mentioned or absent: Jan van de Cappelle, Judith Leyster, Jan Wynants, Jacobus Mancadan, Hendrick Avercamp. Only ten women received their own biographies, fourteen more are mentioned in others'; only three women were illustrated (Anna Maria van Schurman, Maria Sibylla Merian, Johanna Koerten-Blok) (leg 1 F95). He includes notes about publishers and engravers who did not paint at all, and frequently used reprints to document art provenance (leg 1 F96).

## M1. The oval double/triple portrait plate (the family engraving line)

What it is: 43 plates in the Schouburgh, each carrying two or three small oval portraits of artists, drawn by Arnold and engraved on copper by his son Jacobus (with the family -- wife Sara, daughter Antonina -- helping with the oval portraits). How it shows up: the book reads as a gallery wall -- every life opens with a face, and the faces are the proof of the life. The plate is a production system: father draws, son cuts, the whole household inks and proofs. The portraits are in some cases the only surviving likenesses of these painters, so the mechanism doubles as an archive. Watchable behaviour: a steady rhythm of face-face-face, two or three to a plate, the ovals themselves becoming the book's visual unit. (leg 1 M1; leg 1 F37.)

## M2. The theatre (artists brought onstage)

What it is: the book's governing metaphor -- schouburgh = theatre. Artists are "brought onstage" in chronological procession, from Erasmus (c. 1467/9-1536) to Adriaen van den Werff (1659-1722), each life a scene with an entrance (birth/training), action (works, patrons, travel) and exit (death, reputation). How it shows up: the reader watches a 250-year procession file past; the stage framing lets Houbraken mix biography, gossip, art theory and moral judgement in one continuous performance. The title print literalises it: Art restrains Time on a stage set with a cenotaph. (leg 1 M2; leg 1 F36.)

## M3. The case code (UPPERCASE = prominence)

What it is: a typographic ranking system inside the text -- names set in uppercase denote greater prominence, lowercase lesser recognition. How it shows up: scanning a page, the eye is pulled to the capitalised names; the book is a visual hierarchy before it is a text. It is a print-era equivalent of font-weight ranking -- status encoded in letterforms, not in a separate index. (leg 1 M3; leg 1 F41.)

## M4. The correspondence trawl (letters as fieldwork)

What it is: in the last years before the Schouburgh, Houbraken wrote to descendants and students of dead painters asking for background, training, travel, patrons, financial success or failure, and chief achievements. How it shows up: lives built from second-hand testimony, with gaps openly confessed ("potential witnesses have died", "the living have not been forthcoming"), and direct appeals to readers for more information. The mechanism is visible in the book's texture: anecdotes attributed to named informants, dates hedged, portraits missing where no informant could supply one. (leg 1 M4; leg 1 F39/F44.)

## M5. The smuggled digression (theory hidden inside biography)

What it is: Houbraken fused biography and theory in one volume (unlike van Mander, who put theory in a separate book), spacing his theoretical digressions so they would not disrupt the lives -- a digression on ancient sacrificial practices hides inside the Life of Pieter Lastman; the doctrine of choosing what is beautiful hides inside the Life of Rembrandt; a disposition on Envy, the blight of the profession, is illustrated by an attack on Jan de Baen. How it shows up: the reader comes for a life and is ambushed by a lesson; the theory is camouflaged as narrative, which is also how his radical deist ideas survived in print after 1713. (leg 1 M5; leg 1 F93.)

## M6. The emblem as painting manual (Ripa translated into pictures)

What it is: a system for inventing subjects -- Tooneel van sinnebeelden (1700, after Ripa's Iconologia) and Inhoud van 't Sieraad der Afbeelding (1712) give painters a dictionary of personifications, attributes and emblems: a figure + an object + a motto = a meaning a viewer can read. How it shows up: in his own paintings (Eternity with the ouroboros tablet, Wisdom with the "Rerum magistra" scroll) and in the Schouburgh title print (Art's mask of imitatio from Ripa, the ouroboros snake for eternity, Time's scythe and wings). The emblem is a compression algorithm: one figure carries a whole moral sentence. (leg 1 M6; leg 1 F27-F28; leg 2 W14.)

## M7. Physiognomy as evidence (faces that tell the truth)

What it is: the conviction that character, emotion and belief are legible in the face -- learned early (the 1687 Laetsten Duyvels-Dreck title print's background spectators, whose masterfully rendered faces "herald his lasting concern with convincing physiognomy") and applied everywhere. How it shows up: his praise of Rembrandt turns on the variety of facial expressions and the head that "seemed to issue forth and speak to the observer" (Q8); his portraits (the Lady's delicate features against purple curtain, the young man in the kimono) are built around the face as the site of meaning; his Schouburgh portraits are the evidentiary core of each life. (leg 1 M7; leg 1 F72.)

## M8. The title print as thesis (Art restrains Time)

What it is: the engraved frontispiece to vol. I (drawn by Arnold, engraved by Jacob, 157 x 97 mm) is a visual abstract of the whole book: Art, personified, holds one of Time's wings with her left hand while breaking his scythe with her right; the title is engraved on a huge cenotaph behind Time, with Houbraken's name and date on its base; two cupids admire a portrait snatched from Time while a third carries a schematic likeness; an ouroboros (snake biting its tail) stands for eternity. How it shows up: the book's argument -- recording names defeats death -- is stated in one image before a word is read. The accompanying poem spells it out: portraits "quietly secured from the gnawing of worms" so young artists may decorate them with palm and bay, "or carry them to eternity" (Q6). (leg 1 M8.)

## M9. The family workshop and the subscription (self-publishing as a system)

What it is: Houbraken printed the Schouburgh for himself ("Gedrukt voor den autheur"), sold it by subscription at six guilders (three at once, three on delivery), and ran the whole production through his household -- son engraving, daughter and wife helping, widow finishing vol. III after his death. How it shows up: the book's unevenness is the system's fingerprint -- volumes handed to the printer before they were finished, corrections retrofitted into later volumes, the third volume's summary shorter and unthematic because Sara lacked his system. The economics failed (price too low, "ploughed the parched beach"), but the system got 1,207 artists into print in three years. (leg 1 M9; leg 1 F34/F46/F79.)

## M10. Correction in public (the living errata)

What it is: instead of silently fixing mistakes, Houbraken corrects himself in later volumes -- returning to an earlier volume's material when he learns he was misinformed (Van Laer, Torrentius, the de Witte portrait reattributed to van Streek), and publishing appeals for corrections from readers. How it shows up: the three volumes argue with each other; the book is visibly a work in progress, with its own revision history printed inside it. For a generative piece this is a system that visibly revises itself. (leg 1 M10; leg 1 F43-F44.)

## M11. Decorum and dignified emotion (grief without melodrama)

What it is: the Dutch-classicist rule that everything -- style, costume, setting, attribute, emotion, gesture -- must be appropriate to the edifying or heroic subject. In The Sacrifice of Iphigenia: protagonists and onlookers express definite grief, dismay and resignation, but it never degenerates into violent drama; Agamemnon covers his face with his cloak because, following the classical model, the father's grief was so great it could not be depicted; he strikes an august kingly pose; the altar carries authentic-looking motifs; the composition is as animated as the subject allows; the handling is refined and the tonality light. How it shows up: emotion is legible but restrained -- faces turned away, hands covering eyes, bodies posed rather than flailing. The contrast case is Rembrandt, whom Houbraken praises precisely for breaking this rule with inexhaustible variety of expression. (leg 1 M11; leg 2 W5.)

## M12. The learned history painting (rare subjects, small figures, big erudition)

What it is: medium-size canvases with elaborate, complicated compositions and large numbers of small figures, taking rare or recondite subjects from Ovid, the Bible and classical history -- the test of a painter's learning. How it shows up: Pallas Athene (1703) packs the Muses, Apollo and Minerva into a 71 x 96 cm canvas with "convincing movement and emotion"; Iphigenia stages a whole harbour, altar, priests, slaves and spectators in 79.5 x 63.4 cm; the Psyche ceiling (c. 350 x 900 cm) is the same system at architectural scale. The picture is a puzzle the educated viewer decodes -- which god, which text, which attribute. (leg 1 M12; leg 1 F52/F66; leg 2 W5/W12/W19.)

## M13. Mezzotint darkness (light scraped out of black)

What it is: mezzotint works dark-to-light -- the plate is roughened to print a uniform black, and the lights are scraped and burnished out of it. Houbraken made at least three mezzotints; their "peculiar unctuous and swarthy effect" and ability to approach the tonal values of a painting are visible in A Nocturnal Landscape with Shepherd, Nymph and Child, which he also painted in oil (25.1 x 31.5 cm) so the two versions can be compared. How it shows up: night scenes that glow from inside the dark; the print is a painting made by subtraction. (leg 1 M13; leg 1 F71/F65.)

## M14. Invention for the book trade (designs for others' burins)

What it is: the bulk of his graphic output was not autograph prints but preparatory drawings and designs that other engravers cut -- title prints, emblems, Bible illustrations (e.g. The Vocation of St Matthew, c. 1710, a design for an illustrated Bible edition), antiquarian images, and the 425+ illustrations across 31 books counted by Swillens/Horn. How it shows up: Houbraken's line reaches the public through other hands; the "invention" (the composition, the idea) is his, the cutting is someone else's. The Schouburgh portraits invert this: he draws, his son cuts. (leg 1 M14; leg 1 F68-F69; leg 2 W13/W16.)

## M15. The incomplete index and the open list (the book that admits it cannot finish)

What it is: Houbraken repeatedly states the impossibility of a complete list, leaves vol. I's index incomplete, and begs readers for the painters he missed. How it shows up: a reference work that performs its own incompleteness -- gaps, hedges ("to the best of his abilities" for undated artists), and open appeals. The system is honest about its edges, which is why it stayed useful: later scholars (Hofstede de Groot, the RKD) could build on it rather than replace it. (leg 1 M15; leg 1 F41/F44.)

## Works

## W1. Sundial in a landscape with two figures (Op het zien van een zonnewijzer / "On Seeing a Sundial") -- etching, 1682

Date: 1682 (printed in F. van Hoogstraten, De schoole der wereld, 1682). Medium: etching (with letterpress printing, from a book of 36 prints with sentence-images). Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- h 71 mm x w 84 mm (sheet h 158 mm x w 96 mm in the same record; the two figures conflict in the mirror record -- see leg 2 F18). No Rijksmuseum object page was opened; the figures come from an Alamy listing mirroring Rijksmuseum data. Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. What it shows: the earliest dated work in this leg. A column with a sundial in a hilly landscape, a seated figure and a horseman in the background -- an emblem-image in which a moral sentence is carried by a landscape with tiny figures. The mechanism is the sentence-image: text and image as one didactic unit, the picture doing the work of a motto. This is the printmaker's Houbraken, age 22, working inside another author's book -- the first of a lifelong habit of making images that explain, not just depict. (leg 2 W1.)

## W2. Pope on a Pig -- title page for J. Lydius, Laetsten Duyvels-Dreck, 1687

Date: 1687. Medium: engraving/etching, title page. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found in this run. Location: Rijksmuseum (listed among its Houbraken prints); no object page opened. What it shows: early book illustration as polemic: a pope riding a pig as the front door of a theological tract. The mechanism is the emblem as insult -- a single legible, grotesque image that states the book's argument before a word is read. It is the same device as W1's sentence-image, turned satirical, and it prefigures the personification emblems of W14. (leg 2 W2; leg 1 F72.)

## W3. Rest on the Flight into Egypt -- signed and dated 1686

Date: 1686 (signed and dated centre left: "A. Houbraken / 1686"). Medium: oil (support not stated in the source read). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED -- the auction listing (Kunsthaus Lempertz, via Invaluable) was not read end to end and gave no dimensions in the snippet. Location: private collection / art market (Lempertz). What it shows: the earliest dated painting in this leg, and evidence of the Holy Family as what the Lempertz note calls "a favourite subject of Houbraken". The same note warns that the RKD's Hofstede de Groot fiches "attest to several paintings by his hand, but they cannot be clearly assigned" -- i.e. early religious small panels circulate with soft attributions. Mechanism: the small devotional history panel -- a biblical episode reduced to a few figures, close and legible, painted in the fine academic manner his teachers (Levecq, Van Hoogstraten) stood for. (leg 2 W3.)

## W4. The Painter and his Model (De schilder en zijn model) -- c. 1690 / c. 1700

Date: CONFLICT -- Essential Vermeer newsletter 42 (read end to end, 615 lines) captions it "c. 1690"; Erna Kok, "The female nude from life" (2011, read in part, lines 0-419 of 1,900) dates it "in 1700" in the text and "c. 1700" in the figure caption. See leg 2 F1. Medium: oil on panel. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 28.5 x 19 cm (Essential Vermeer). No Rijksmuseum object page was opened. Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam -- BUT the Rijksmuseum panel is catalogued as a copy after Arnold Houbraken ("Arnold van Houbraken (kopie naar) -- De schilder en zijn model 1690", Wikimedia Commons file name; Alamy caption: "copy after a mezzo-tint by Nicolaes Verkolje (1673-1746) after Arnold Houbraken"). The original's whereabouts are not established in this run. See C571. What it shows: a painter behind his easel portraying a nude young woman, her back to the beholder, leaning with her knee on a stool, clothes in a heap, slippers cast aside; an old man grins at the viewer; a monkey peers from under the stool; a drawing of an embracing nude couple is pinned to the window shutter. Kok's reading (the only close reading this leg found): the painter sits too close to judge proportions, so this is not a realistic studio record but a staged invitation -- the private studio turned into a public stage, the beholder made a legitimised Peeping Tom, the monkey marking sin. The anecdote behind it is Houbraken's own Life of Rembrandt story of a pupil who shut himself behind a cloth with a female model ("Surely now it's even as if we are Adam and Eve in Paradise"). Mechanism: the studio as theatre -- the act of painting staged for a viewer who is put inside the voyeur's seat -- and the artist-model topos (Apelles/Campaspe, Pygmalion), which Kok traces through the article. Note: no Houbraken Pygmalion painting was found in this run; Pygmalion enters only as this topos (see leg 2 F9). (leg 2 W4.)

## W5. The Sacrifice of Iphigenia -- c. 1690-1700

Date: c. 1690-1700 (Rijksmuseum dating as mirrored); single-year "1700" in several mirrors; wahooart says "around 1690". See leg 2 F14. Medium: oil paint (painting). Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- height 80 cm, width 64 cm (depth 7 cm in the Artera mirror record -- a frame depth, presumably; leg 2 F19). Multiple mirrors of Rijksmuseum data agree on 80 x 64 cm, but no Rijksmuseum object page was opened in this run. Horn, citing the Rijksmuseum, gives 79.5 x 63.4 cm (leg 1 F53/C9); use the Horn/Rijksmuseum figure. Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (inv. SK-A-4942, acquired 1998; auctioned Sotheby's London 3 July 1996, no. 177). What it shows: the high priest Kalchas about to sacrifice Iphigenia to Artemis, whose statue stands on the altar; Iphigenia kneels with bare torso; Agamemnon, left, holds a hand before his eyes; spectators left and right turn their heads away. Subject from Ovid's Metamorphoses: the priest Kalchas is about to sacrifice Iphigenia to appease Artemis so the Athenian fleet might leave Aulis; black slaves at right suggest the exotic southern harbour; background architecture shows "the tooth of time". Mechanism: the small history panel as frieze -- a mythological climax arranged as a row of reacting faces around one kneeling body, the statue of the goddess looming over the human action. The turned-away heads do the emotional work: the horror is shown by refusal to look. This is the mature Dordrecht history piece, the mode White Rose calls "refined academic manner" and the V&A calls "the classical grace of idealised figures, clear contours and delicate colours of late Dutch classicism". (leg 2 W5; leg 1 F53/M11.)

## W6. Commemoration of King Mausolus by Queen Artemisia -- undated (Dordrecht/Amsterdam period)

Date: undated in every source found; placed here by style and by its pairing with W5 as a small classical history panel. No date is asserted. Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 66 x 79 cm (geerts.com, read end to end; repeated by kuadros/allpaintingsstore/wikioo mirrors). Location: private collection. What it shows: the funeral ceremony at Halicarnassus for King Mausolus, with Queen Artemisia (the seated woman on the throne at centre, per the kuadros note -- unconfirmed) presiding over a crowded classical rite. Mechanism: the ceremony as catalogue -- a historical event staged as a procession of costumes, gestures and architecture, the painter showing off his antiquarian knowledge (the same learned apparatus that fills the Schouburgh's digressions). The picture is reproduced in the EN Wikipedia article as the lead image of Houbraken's painted oeuvre. (leg 2 W6; leg 1 F58.)

## W7. Portrait of a Man (Rudolf van Loen?) -- c. 1700

Date: c. 1700 (Wikimedia Commons Stadel list; White Rose compares it with the c. 1700-1705 Portrait of a Lady). Medium: oil on wood (Stadel page: "Oil on wood, reverse original, beveled on all sides"). Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 42.7 x 33.8 cm, inv. no. 1122 (Jochen Sander and Bodo Brinkmann, Niederlandische Gemalde vor 1800 im Stadel, 1995, p. 36, fig. 51, as cited by White Rose). The Stadel object page itself, read end to end (36 lines), gives NO dimensions. See leg 2 F4. Location: Stadel Museum, Frankfurt am Main, inv. 1122. What it shows: a man's portrait, signed on the left edge at elbow height "Houbraken". The sitter's identity as Rudolf van Loen is uncertain -- the Stadel titles it with a question mark. Mechanism: the small oval-format gentleman's portrait at the scale of a cabinet picture (42 x 34 cm), the sitter set against a landscape with a curtain -- the same kit of parts as W10/W11, but here the sitter is a named (if doubtfully named) Dordrecht notable rather than an unknown lady. It anchors the claim that portraiture was Houbraken's Dordrecht bread-and-butter. (leg 2 W7.)

## W8. Dido Conducting Aeneas into Carthage -- c. 1700

Date: c. 1700 ("most likely depicted around 1700", V&A, read end to end). Medium: oil on panel (signed panel; the V&A copy is oil on copper). Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- "approximately the same" as the V&A copy, approx. 60.9 x 82 cm (V&A dimensions are for the copy, taken from Kauffmann 1973, and are NOT Arnold's dimensions; see leg 2 F15). Location: John and Mable Ringling Museum of Art, Sarasota, SN273 (the signed original). A larger third version on canvas was sold at Sotheby's London, 12 April 1978, lot 132. The V&A holds an early-18th-century exact copy after Arnold (oil on copper, monogram WM, acc. 1362-1869, bequeathed by Rev. Chauncy Hare Townshend, 1868). What it shows: before the gate of Carthage, Dido conducts Aeneas into the city; merchants and people welcome the hero and his companions, who bring presents (the confusion on the left half of the picture, per the V&A). Virgil, Aeneid I, 613-642. Mechanism: the arrival as civic virtue -- the V&A's own reading: Aeneas's virtue lies in his forthcoming renunciation of Dido to fulfil his destiny and found Rome; the picture belongs to the large group of republican Roman imagery "highly appreciated in Amsterdam from the mid 17th century onwards" as virtutis exempla, models of civic and personal virtue. The existence of an exact contemporary copy plus a larger canvas version shows the composition was a repeatable product, not a one-off -- the small history panel as a reproducible design. (leg 2 W8.)

## W9. Portrait of Josina van Loon (1674-1736) -- dated 1701

Date: 1701 (White Rose, read end to end, citing E.W. Moes, Iconographia Batava, no. 4609). Medium: oil on copper. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 21.5 x 18 cm, inv. no. 2579 (White Rose citation of Moes; no Museum Van Loon object page was opened). Location: Museum Van Loon, Amsterdam. What it shows: a miniature-scale portrait on copper of a named Amsterdam patrician woman -- the smallest portrait format in this leg, and the only dated portrait (1701), which fixes the Dordrecht portrait mode in time. Mechanism: portrait as jewel -- oil on copper at 21.5 x 18 cm, a portable likeness for a private interior, the opposite pole from the 350 x 900 cm ceiling of W19. The copper support is itself a marker of the fine, smooth, enamel-like finish the academic manner prized. (leg 2 W9.)

## W10. Portrait of a Lady -- c. 1700-1705

Date: c. 1700-1705 (White Rose, read end to end). Medium: oil on panel, oval. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 42.9 x 34.3 cm (16.9 x 13.5 in.), signed "A. Houbraken." (lower right). White Rose is a dealer page read end to end; under the dimension rule a dealer page does not verify. Location: private collection (provenance: Lady Francis Vyvyan, London; her sale, Christie's, 23 June 1950, lot 13; private collection, Belgium). What it shows: an unknown Dordrecht gentlewoman before an elegant Mediterranean landscape, a curtain in exuberant purple behind her, delicate features set against it; her hands hold a rich scarf billowing in the wind -- what White Rose calls "a fantasy garment to add a touch of whimsy and movement in portraits, frequently employed by painters". Mechanism: the portrait with a moving prop -- the still sitter animated by one piece of wind-blown cloth, the landscape and curtain doing the work of status while the scarf does the work of life. White Rose dates it to the Dordrecht years and compares it directly with W7. (leg 2 W10; leg 1 F63.)

## W11. Portrait of Catharina van Elsdijk (b. c. 1680) -- c. 1700-1705

Date: c. 1700-1705 (White Rose, read end to end). Medium: oil on panel, oval. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 43 x 34.5 cm, signed "A. Houbrake." (lower right). Dealer page, not a museum page. Location: private collection (provenance: Willem Jan Kruseman (1849-1918), Middelburg, by descent until 2014; private collection, The Netherlands). Sold by White Rose. What it shows: Catharina van Elsdijk, who married the Middelburg merchant Jacobus Berdenis on 12 December 1707 and moved to Zeeland; her portrait -- painted as a stand-alone work, not a pendant, which White Rose says explains why the sitters do not face each other -- served as the model in size and format for her husband's companion portrait by the Middelburg painter D. Koene, who "tried to emulate the style of Houbraken, but it is obvious he did not reach this master's elegance and refinement". Mechanism: the portrait as template -- one painter's oval format becoming the measure another painter must copy, so that Houbraken's portraiture literally sets the standard for a provincial colleague. Seen by E.W. Moes, documented Iconografisch Bureau no. IB 12557. (leg 2 W11.)

## W12. Pallas Athene Visiting Apollo on the Parnassus -- 1703

Date: 1703 (EN Wikipedia; geerts.com). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 71 x 96 cm (geerts.com, read end to end; no Dordrechts Museum object page was opened). Location: Dordrechts Museum. What it shows: the goddess Minerva (Pallas Athene), shield bearing the Medusa head, visits Apollo -- shown with a golden halo as sun god -- and the nine Muses. Geerts notes the story as told by Ovid is Minerva visiting the Muses on Mount Helicon, "not on Parnassus as the title suggests" -- the title misnames the mountain (leg 1 F54/C17). Complex but brilliantly resolved composition with convincing movement and emotion and harmonious colour; one of thirty paintings by Houbraken and colleagues that his widow sold on 17 July 1720 to help fund vol. III. Mechanism: the mythological assembly -- gods and Muses arranged as a learned conversation piece, the picture's subject being listening itself (Minerva came to hear the Muses' songs and stories). It is the largest dated Dordrecht history painting in this leg and the clearest statement of the academic programme: classical grace, clear contours, delicate colour, a subject that requires the viewer to know Ovid. (leg 2 W12; leg 1 F54.)

## W13. The Vocation of St. Matthew -- c. 1710

Date: c. 1710 (geerts.com, read end to end). Medium: oil on panel. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- 41.3 x 49.5 cm (geerts.com; no museum page opened). Location: Dordrechts Museum. What it shows: Matthew 9:9-10 -- "As Jesus went on from there, he saw a man named Matthew sitting at the tax collector's booth. 'Follow me,' he told him, and Matthew got up and followed him", then dinner at Matthew's house with tax collectors and sinners. Painted, per geerts, "in masterly fashion mainly in brown tints" and "made in preparation for an illustrated Bible edition in which many prints after designs by Houbraken were used". Mechanism: the painting as print-design -- a panel conceived as the model for a book illustration, the brown monochrome palette suiting translation into black-and-white engraving. This is the hinge between Houbraken the painter and Houbraken the print-designer: the picture is made to be copied. (leg 2 W13; leg 1 F56/M14.)

## W14. Forty-One Various Emblems (Een-en-veertigh stuks, verscheydene Sinnebeelden) -- undated (early 18th century?)

Date: undated in every source found; publisher Leonard Schenk. Placed here by the Amsterdam-period book work; no date is asserted. Medium: engravings/etchings after Arnold's own designs; printmaker Arnold Houbraken, publisher Leonard Schenk. Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED -- sheets vary; no single sheet's dimensions come from a museum page read end to end. (A related Rijksmuseum sheet, Vrouwfiguur met palet wijzend naar portret van Arnold Houbraken, 1710s, is given as 180 x 135 mm in a mirror of Rijksmuseum data -- that is a different, allegorical sheet, listed in Beyond the 20.) Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (series held there). What it shows: forty-one personifications -- Married State, Poetry, Prudence, Purity, Self-Esteem (Eygen Liefde), Beauty, Wisdom, Music, Contentment, Bliss, Perfection, Prosperity, Modesty, Gratitude, Love, plus the busts of Democritus and Heraclitus -- each an abstract noun given one body and its attributes. Mechanism: the personification system -- the emblem book as a visual dictionary, one figure per idea, each instantly legible by attribute (this is the direct continuation of W1's sentence-image and of the 1712 Inhoud van 't Sieraad, W20). For the study's purposes this is Houbraken's most generative mechanism: a finite kit of labelled figures that can be recombined into new scenes. (leg 2 W14; leg 1 M6.)

## W15. Erichthonius Found by the Three Daughters of Cecrops (Quantum agnovisse paenitet) -- undated

Date: undated. The SuperStock mirror of Rijksmuseum data gives only "Amsterdam, Dordrecht, 1670-1719" -- his working life, not a date. Placed here as a mature print; no date is asserted. Medium: etching on paper, after Arnold's own design; printmaker and publisher Arnold Houbraken. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- height 367 mm x width 276 mm (mirror of Rijksmuseum data; no Rijksmuseum object page opened). Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. What it shows: three women around a large woven basket: one kneels with her back partly to the viewer, hands on the basket rim; another leans forward peering in; a third stands draped, reaching toward a wall; inside, a small infant with a snake's tail instead of legs stretches its arms toward the kneeling woman. Below the image a line in Latin: Quantum agnovisse paenitet ("How much it pains to have known"). Mechanism: the reveal as fright -- the basket as a closed box whose opening is the picture's event, the three reactions (kneel, peer, recoil) staged around it like the turned heads of W5. The Latin motto makes it an emblem-print in the W1/W14 line: image plus sentence, the picture incomplete without its caption. (leg 2 W15; leg 1 F70.)

## W16. Aeneas and the Sibyl in the Underworld -- undated (drawing)

Date: n.d. (Met). Medium: pen and brown ink, brush and gray wash, indented for transfer. Dimensions: VERIFIED -- sheet 3 3/4 x 5 3/16 in. (9.6 x 13.1 cm). Met Museum object page read end to end (46 lines). Location: Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York. Object number 2006.301; The Elisha Whittelsey Collection, The Elisha Whittelsey Fund, 2006; Drawings and Prints. What it shows: Aeneas, golden bough in hand, with the Cumaean Sibyl at the monster-guarded cave that leads to Charon's ferry (Aeneid VI). The Historian's Hut (read end to end, 40 lines) retells the episode: the Sibyl's erratic prophecies, the golden bough as key, the ferry across the Styx, Cerberus, Minos, Tartarus and Elysium. Mechanism: the drawing as transfer design -- "indented for transfer" means the sheet was made to be copied onto a plate or another support; the image is a working stage, not a finished display piece. At 9.6 x 13.1 cm it is the smallest work in this leg and the clearest evidence of Houbraken's draughtsman-to-print pipeline (V&A: "a prolific draughtsman who supplied many designs and etchings for book illustrations and a number of his drawings were engraved by his contemporaries"). (leg 2 W16.)

## W17. Portrait of Arnold Houbraken (author portrait for the Schouburgh) -- 1718

Date: 1718. Medium: line engraving by Jacobus Houbraken, after Arnold's own self-portrait design. (The engraver is the son; the image is the father's self-presentation.) Dimensions: VERIFIED -- 6 3/8 in. x 4 in. (162 mm x 101 mm) paper size. NPG D27613 page read end to end (52 lines); duplicate impression NPG D27614 read end to end (54 lines), same data. NOTE: Hendrik Horn's Houbraken Celebrated (search snippet) gives the plate as 158 x 105 mm, "In: Arnold Houbraken, De groote schouburgh, III, 1721, before page one" -- plate size vs paper size; see leg 2 F7. Location: National Portrait Gallery, London. NPG D27613 and D27614, Reference Collection; given by Mary Elizabeth Stopford (nee Fleming), daughter of compiler William Fleming MD, 1931. NPG lists Arnold as "Sitter in 2 portraits". What it shows: Arnold himself, engraved by his twenty-year-old son, placed at the front of the third volume of the Schouburgh -- the author staging his own likeness as the frontispiece to his theatre of other painters' lives. Mechanism: the self-portrait as frontispiece -- the biographer putting himself on stage first, so that the book's authority has a face. It is also the father-to-son transfer made visible: Arnold draws, Jacobus engraves, and the son's burin carries the father's face into the book. (leg 2 W17; leg 1 M1/M9.)

## W18. De groote schouburgh der Nederlantsche konstschilders en schilderessen (The Great Theatre of Netherlandish Painters and Paintresses) -- vols. I-III, 1718, 1719, 1721

Date: Vol. I 1718; vol. II 1719; vol. III 1721 (posthumous -- Arnold died 14 October 1719; vol. III was printed for his widow, "Gedrukt voor de Weduwe des Autheurs"). Self-published in Amsterdam ("gedrukt voor den autheur"). Medium: three printed volumes, 8vo, with engraved portraits and plates. Plate counts differ by source -- see leg 2 F11: 43 plates with double/triple portraits (Horn); 49 full-page plates (auction catalogue: frontispiece, engraved dedication, author's portrait, 46 plates mostly portraits, plus 7 small engravings); 45 original illustrations (Houbraken Translated foreword). Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- first edition collation [12]-381-[9], [4]-361-[7], [4]-408-[7] pp. (auction catalogue, not read end to end); second edition (The Hague, 1753) 20 x 13 cm (auction listing). No copy measured from a library page read end to end. Location: many copies; the RKD holds the rare-books set scanned for Houbraken Translated; the text is in the DBNL Basic Library (1000 most important works in Dutch literature). One first-edition set's details are quoted from the Invaluable auction listing (not read end to end). What it shows: 1,207 artists named or discussed (RKD count via Houbraken Translated: 1,015 Dutch or Flemish painters and glass painters, 192 others -- engravers, sculptors, architects, foreign artists); 521 works named by Houbraken could be identified and are illustrated in the translation; 294 collectors identified. The biographies run from Erasmus (c. 1467/9-1536, "who is also to have painted") to Adriaen van der Werff (1659-1722). Mechanism: the theatre -- lives brought "onstage" in chronological order, each with an engraved portrait as its entrance, the whole framed as a sequel to Karel van Mander's Schilder-Boeck. The book is Houbraken's largest work by every measure (years, pages, people, plates) and the one that turned a painter's workshop knowledge -- shop practice, travel, prices, patrons, successes and failures -- into a public, checkable record. The Houbraken Translated foreword (read end to end, 87 lines) stresses what the book is beyond a lexicon: art theory, shop practice, religion and philosophy, "loaded with learned antiquarian information intended for young history painters". (leg 2 W18; leg 1 F34-F51/M2.)

## W19. Psyche Taken to Heaven and Marries Amor -- undated (ceiling painting)

Date: undated in every source found; placed late by scale and by the Amsterdam-period ceiling commissions. No date is asserted. Medium: oil on canvas, ceiling painting. Dimensions: reported-but-unverified -- c. 350 x 900 cm (geerts.com, read end to end; no museum page opened). Location: Dordrechts Museum (originally in a house on the Groenmarkt, Dordrecht; part of the museum's collection since 1944). What it shows: Venus, jealous of the beautiful mortal Psyche, sends Cupid to make her love the ugliest man on earth; Cupid and Psyche fall for each other instead; Jupiter takes pity, grants Psyche immortality, and the gods celebrate the wedding. TITLE FLAG (leg 2 F10): the V&A Dido page (read end to end) mentions a ceiling panel "Eros conducting Psyche into the Parnassus", also housed in the Dordrecht Museum -- this may be the same ceiling under a different title, or a second Psyche ceiling. Not resolved in this run. What it shows about mechanism: the ceiling as sky -- the largest work in this leg by two orders of magnitude (3.5 x 9 metres against the 28.5 x 19 cm of W4), a domestic ceiling turned into an Olympian wedding feast. It proves Houbraken could work at architectural scale, not only at cabinet scale, and it is the physical opposite of the small history panel: same mythology, same academic grace, but painted to be looked up at from a room below. (leg 2 W19; leg 1 F57/M12.)

## W20. Inhoud van 't Sieraad der Afbeelding (Contents of the Ornament of Depiction) -- 1712

Date: 1712, published inside Des menschen begin, midden en einde (Man's Beginning, Middle and End). Medium: emblem book / instructive manual for artists (printed text with engraved emblems). Dimensions: NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions or collation found in this run. Location: copies in libraries (DBNL; Google Books); no copy examined end to end. What it shows: Houbraken's first attempt at an instructive manual for artists (EN Wikipedia, read end to end): a guide of possible painting themes -- i.e. a book that tells a young painter what to paint. Mechanism: the theme-book -- the emblem system of W14 turned into a curriculum. It is the hinge between the early emblem prints (W1, W2) and the Schouburgh (W18): first he catalogues subjects, then he catalogues lives. The same year (1712) he published the Philalethes Brieven, the deist letters that got him censured by the Amsterdam Church Council in 1713 and sent him to London for nine months (Houbraken Translated foreword) -- the manual and the heresy are the same year's work. (leg 2 W20; leg 1 F28/M6.)

## Beyond the 20

Paintings: 1. Juno, Apollo and Io -- oil on canvas, 53.3 x 50.8 cm, private collection (geerts.com). Title variant: the same picture circulates as Jupiter, Juno and Io (wahooart/1st-art-gallery). See leg 2 F3. 2. Eternity seated by a five (Eternity seated by a five, holding a tablet with the ouroboros in her left hand, her right hand leaning on a sphere, as Wisdom stands nearby) -- oil on canvas, 168 x 117.8 cm, private collection (geerts.com). Wisdom leans on a staff encircled by a scroll inscribed "Rerum magistra". The title is garbled; do not reproduce it as given without verification (leg 1 C18). 3. Portrait of a girl -- oil on canvas, 46.3 x 36 cm, Dordrechts Museum (geerts.com). 4. Portrait of a young man (three-quarter-length, yellow silk kimono with red lining, earring, leaning on a stone plinth, garden beyond) -- oil on panel, 47.9 x 38.7 cm, private collection (geerts.com). 5. Death of Dido -- Dordrechts Museum, "recently acquired" per the V&A Dido page (read end to end); no date or dimensions found. 6. Eros conducting Psyche into the Parnassus -- ceiling panel, Dordrechts Museum (V&A Dido page); possibly the same work as W19 under another title -- see leg 2 F10. 7. Susannah and the Elders -- Manchester Art Gallery, inv. 1931.128 (Wikimedia Commons list of Susanna paintings); no date or dimensions found; no Art UK/Manchester page URL surfaced. 8. A young scholar reading in a study -- signed lower left "A Houbraken", oil on oak panel, unframed 23 x 19.3 cm (framed 29.5 x 26.2 cm), Sotheby's (via Invaluable; listing not read end to end). 9. Christ and the Samaritan Woman at the Well (Le Christ et la Samaritaine au puits) -- oil on canvas marouflaged on oak panel, signed lower right (Invaluable listing; no dimensions in snippet). 10. The Fable of Alphaeus and Arethusa (Invaluable listing; no date, medium or location found). 11. Death of Lucretia -- attributed to Arnold Houbraken (Invaluable listing; attribution only). 12. Vertumnus and Pomona (Invaluable listing; no date, medium or location found in this run). 13. The Virginia killed through the streets of Rome -- "painted around the turn of the 18th century" per wahooart (dealer copy, not a source to trust for dating); no location found. 14. Wife of King Candaulus as Revealed to Gyges (reproduction listings only; no date, medium or location found). 15. Holy Family -- listed in Google Arts and Culture's Houbraken entity (via Wikidata); no details found. Cf. W3, Rest on the Flight into Egypt, which the Lempertz note calls one of several Holy Family pictures. 16. St. Jerome -- listed in Google Arts and Culture's Houbraken entity; no details found. 17. Pharaoh's Daughter Finding of Moses / Elijah, a pair -- Bukowskis auction, 14-16 June 2023 (Art.Salon listing; no dimensions in snippet).

Prints, drawings and book work: 18. Vrouwfiguur met palet wijzend naar portret van Arnold Houbraken (Woman with a palette pointing to the portrait of Arnold Houbraken) -- engraving, 1710s, height 180 mm x width 135 mm, Rijksmuseum (mirror of Rijksmuseum data). An allegorical frontispiece-like sheet: Painting herself presents the author's portrait; a monkey painting with a broken chain at the bottom is read (by the Artera mirror's "curator") as emancipation from the academies -- that reading is the mirror's, not a museum's. 19. Sleeping Nymph with a Dog Spied by a Satyr (Quam Meminisse Juvat) -- etching, Theater of Emblems series, Rijksmuseum (meisterdrucke listing). 20. Personification of Painting on a Pedestal -- title page of the Theatre of Emblems (meisterdrucke listing). 21. Personification of Love and Laureled Christ -- title page for J. Oldenborgh, Explanation of the Song of Songs, 1691 (meisterdrucke listing). 22. Shepherdess and Shepherd with Their Child (Herderin en herder met hun kind) -- print, Rijksmuseum (meisterdrucke listing). 23. Individual sheets of the Forty-One Emblems beyond W14's survey: Personification of the Married State, Personification of Poetry, Personification of Prudence, Personification of Purity, Personification of Self-Esteem (Eygen Liefde), Personification of Beauty, Personification of Wisdom, Personification of Music, Personification of Contentment, Personification of Bliss, Personification of Perfection, Personification of Prosperity, Personification of Modesty, Personified Gratitude, Bust of Democritus, Bust of Heraclitus -- all Rijksmuseum, publisher Leonard Schenk. 24. Sundial in a landscape with two figures -- see W1 (the 1682 De schoole der wereld plate; listed here again only to note the book held 36 such prints). 25. Aen den heere Jakob Zeeus, den Wolf in 't schaepsvel ter drukpersse bestellende (1711, in De wolf in 't schaepsvel) -- book verse/illustration work (EN Wikipedia bibliography). 26. Philalethes Brieven (1712) -- theological letters, not an artwork, but the publication that got Houbraken censured in 1713 and sent to London for nine months (Houbraken Translated foreword). 27. The Schouburgh artist portraits as a body: 43 plates with double or triple portraits drawn by Arnold and engraved by his son Jacobus and seven others (Horn); e.g. Portrait of Ludolf Bakhuizen, engraving by Jacobus Houbraken after Arnold, 1718-1721, British Museum Sheepshanks.427 (BM page returned boilerplate only -- not read end to end; description from search snippet). 28. Dido Conducting Aeneas into Carthage -- V&A early-18th-century exact copy after Arnold, oil on copper, approx. 60.9 x 82 cm, acc. 1362-1869 (NOT by Arnold; listed here to keep it distinct from W8).

NOT Arnold's, excluded (see leg 2 F8): Het Amsterdamsche Kermis Feest -- engraving after Cornelis Troost, 41.5 x 49.5 cm, engraved by Jacobus Houbraken (not Arnold) as one of the 32 Troost scenes; the Invaluable Arnold page lists it under Arnold's name, which is a misattribution.

## Evolution

Early -- Dordrecht apprenticeship and book work (1672-c. 1690). Houbraken is sent first to learn threadtwisting from Johannes de Haan, who introduces him to engraving; two years with Willem van Drielenburch (through the rampjaar 1672), nine months with Jacobus Levecq, four years with Samuel van Hoogstraten; enters the Dordrecht Guild of St Luke in 1678; marries Sara Sasbout/Souborg in 1685 (see leg 2 F5). The work of this phase is graphic and small: the 1682 sundial etching inside another author's emblem book (W1), title pages and polemical frontispieces (W2, 1687; the 1691 Song of Songs title page), and the first dated painting, the 1686 Rest on the Flight (W3). The mechanism is already fixed: image + sentence, the picture as an argument. He is a printmaker who paints, not yet a painter who writes. (Note: leg 2's Evolution also states a 1680 England trip; that claim is single-source and unverified -- see C570. This study does not state it.)

Middle -- Dordrecht portraiture and the small history panel (c. 1690-1709). Portraits are the living: the oval cabinet portraits (W7, W10, W11, and the dated 1701 Josina van Loon on copper, W9), all 42-43 x 34-35 cm, all built from the same kit -- landscape, curtain, billowing scarf, smooth enamel finish. Alongside them, the small history panel matures: The Painter and his Model (W4, c. 1690/1700), Sacrifice of Iphigenia (W5, c. 1690-1700), Commemoration of King Mausolus (W6), the Ringling Dido (W8, c. 1700), and the dated Pallas Athene (W12, 1703, 71 x 96 cm -- the largest dated Dordrecht canvas in this leg). The scale stays small (28-96 cm), the subjects classical or biblical, the manner "refined academic" -- idealised figures, clear contours, delicate colour. The studio picture (W4) is the hinge work of the phase: a painter painting a nude model, the beholder invited in as voyeur, the whole thing justified by a Rembrandt anecdote Houbraken himself wrote down. Painting and writing are now feeding each other.

Late -- Amsterdam, myth, ceilings and the book (1709-1719). Around 1709 he moves with his family to Amsterdam, where the White Rose note says he had found "a wealthy Maecenas, Jonas Witsen"; the V&A gives 1709/10 (see leg 2 F6; see C576). In Amsterdam, White Rose says, "he was able to concentrate on mythological subjects, painted in a refined academic manner, which were highly esteemed during the period and the ultimate test of a painter's ability". The dated evidence: The Vocation of St. Matthew c. 1710 (W13), explicitly made as a design for Bible illustrations -- painting in service of print; the Psyche ceiling (W19, c. 350 x 900 cm) at architectural scale; the emblem book Inhoud van 't Sieraad (W20, 1712) and the Philalethes Brieven (1712), the deist letters that get him censured and sent to London for nine months in 1713, where he sees 32 Van Dycks at Philip Wharton's house; the Forty-One Emblems (W14) and the Erichthonius etching (W15) as the mature print work; the transfer drawing Aeneas and the Sibyl (W16) as the draughtsman's working stage; and finally the Schouburgh itself (W18, 1718-1721), in which the painter becomes the biographer and the engraver is his son. He dies 14 October 1719 with vol. III unfinished; his widow publishes it in 1721 and his son finishes the proofs. The arc is from image-as-sentence (1682) to lives-as-theatre (1718-21): the emblem habit -- one figure, one idea, one caption -- scaled up until the idea is a whole life and the caption is a biography.

What does not change: the small scale of the easel work (almost everything is under a metre), the classical/biblical subject matter, the smooth academic finish, and the dependence on print -- designs made to be engraved, paintings made to be copied, a book made to be consulted. What changes is the audience: from a Dordrecht buyer of portraits, to an Amsterdam patron of mythology, to the whole Republic of readers. (leg 2 Evolution.)

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- The Amsterdam move (leg 1 C1; see C576). 1709 (EN Wikipedia "around 1709"; Brill ch. 9; NL Wikipedia c. 1709, at Jonas Witsen's invitation; FR Wikipedia Amsterdam 1709-1713) vs 1709/1710 (DE Wikipedia, settled 1709/1710 on the Prinsengracht; V&A 1709/10) vs 1710 (British Museum, per the #542 file). No source in this run supports 1710 as a standalone date -- confirmed again in this run. The 1707 date belongs to his son Jacobus's move to Amsterdam (EN Wikipedia "Jacobus Houbraken"; Britannica/ADB), and is frequently confused with Arnold's move; geerts.com gives 1709 for Jacobus. Unresolved; use "c. 1709".
- Children: ten vs nine (leg 1 C2). EN/DE Wikipedia and anticstore: ten children. LibraryThing blurb of Horn's The Golden Age Revisited: "his wife Sara and their nine children". Only three children are named anywhere in the sources read. Use ten, with the nine-count flagged as a Horn-blurb outlier (possibly counting surviving children).
- Mother's name: Geertruyt Aertsdr vs Truycken Gudde (leg 1 C3). Horn (2023, p. 37, citing Dordrecht archivist van Dalen): mother Geertruyt Aertsdr (d. 1679), never mentioned by Houbraken. Anticstore: "son of the tailor Jan Jansz Houbraken and Truycken Gudde". Unresolved in this run; Horn's archival citation is stronger, but "Truycken" may be a diminutive/variant.
- Wife's name: Sara Sasbout vs Sara Sasbout Souburg vs Sara Souborg (leg 1 C4). EN/NL Wikipedia: Sara Sasbout. DE Wikipedia: Sara Sasbout Souburg (1662-1729), daughter of Jacob Sasbout Souburg. Anticstore: Sara Souborg. These are variants of one name, not different people; "Souburg" is the father's surname. Use "Sara Sasbout (Souburg)".
- Jacobus's birth: 25 December 1698 vs baptised 17 December 1698 (leg 1 C5). EN Wikipedia "Jacobus Houbraken": born 25 December 1698. DE Wikipedia "Arnold Houbraken": Jacobus baptised Dordrecht 17 December 1698. A baptism eight days before a birth is impossible; one of the two dates is wrong (or the 25 December is a traditional/confused date). The #545 study settled this as "December 1698" (the exact birth date unknown); this study follows #545, not re-litigating here.
- The 1680 England trip (leg 1 C6; see C570). Claimed only by anticstore ("In 1680 Houbraken travelled to England"). No other source in this run mentions a 1680 trip; all agree on the 1713 London flight. Single-source, unverified -- not stated as fact in this study.
- Stichtelyke zinnebeelden: written 1714 vs published 1723 (leg 1 C7). DE Wikipedia: made 1714 with Gesine Brit. NL Wikipedia: the 57-print book of 1723 also stood in Gesina Brit's name. Horn 2023, p. 218: written in 1714, still unpublished at his death, published well after. These reconcile as written 1714 / published 1723 -- not a true dispute; the study gives both dates with their roles.
- Plates/illustrations in the Schouburgh: 43 vs 45 (leg 1 C8; leg 2 F11). Horn 2023 (Introduction): "forty-three plates with double or triple portraits ... as well as by seven other plates" = 50 plates total, 43 of them portrait plates. Houbraken Translated Foreword: "all the 45 original illustrations of De groote schouburgh are also included". EN Wikipedia "Schouburgh" lists plates A-R (vol. I, 18 plates), A-K (vol. II, 10 plates), A-P (vol. III, 16 plates) = 44 lettered portrait plates, plus the title print. An auction catalogue counts 49 full-page plates + 7 small engravings. The counts differ by what is counted (portrait plates vs all illustrations vs lettered plates). Do not quote a single number without saying what it counts.
- Sacrifice of Iphigenia dimensions: 79.5 x 63.4 cm (Horn, citing Rijksmuseum SK-A-4942) vs 80 x 64 cm (topimpressionists/wahooart search snippets) (leg 1 C9). Rounding; use the Horn/Rijksmuseum figure.
- Pupil Jan Claesz Rietschoof (leg 1 C10). FR Wikipedia infobox lists him as Houbraken's pupil; EN Wikipedia's RKD-based list (Balen, Graham, Jacobus) does not. Single source (FR Wikipedia); RKD check needed before asserting.
- Danish Wikipedia death date error (leg 1 C11). DA Wikipedia infobox says died 10 February 1719 (aged 58), while its own article text says 14 October 1719. The infobox is wrong; 14 October 1719 is unanimous elsewhere. Do not use the DA infobox date.
- Name spellings (leg 1 C12). Levecq / Leveck / Lavecq; Drielenburch / Drielenburg / van Drillenburg; Stellingwerf / Stellingwerff; Sasbout / Souburg / Souborg. All variants of the same people; the study picks one spelling and notes variants.
- "Aert" vs "Arnold" (leg 1 C13; see C574). Horn's archival finding (baptised Aert, 30 June 1680) against the universal "Arnold" of all other sources. The study mentions the baptismal name Aert as a Horn discovery, not as a replacement for Arnold.
- Artists named in the Schouburgh: 1,207 vs "about 500 biographies" (leg 1 C14; leg 2 F12). Foreword (RKD count): 1,207 named or discussed. JA Wikipedia: "about 500 biographies". An auction catalogue says "some 550 Dutch and Flemish artists". These count different things (named/discussed vs full biographies). Use the RKD figure with the definition attached.
- Second edition date (leg 1 C15). NL/FR Wikipedia: second edition 1753 (The Hague). EN Wikipedia "Schouburgh" mentions a 1756 edition with an improved index. Both may be true (1753 second edition; 1756 reissue with better index) but no source in this run confirms the relationship. Flag.
- Antonina's husband's name and dates (leg 1 C16). EN Wikipedia: "Jacob Stellingwerf" (goldsmith, per anticstore). NL Wikipedia: Jacobus Stellingwerff (1667-1727), topographical draughtsman. DE Wikipedia: Jacobus Stellingwerf (1667-1727). "Goldsmith" (anticstore) vs "topographical draughtsman" (NL) conflict -- the NL/DE identification as a draughtsman is better sourced; anticstore's "goldsmith" is likely an error. Flag.
- Pallas Athene title vs subject (leg 1 C17). The painting is titled "Pallas Athene Visiting Apollo on the Parnassus", but Ovid's story has Minerva visiting the Muses on Mount Helicon, not Parnassus (geerts.com). The title is a traditional misnomer; the study notes it.
- Eternity seated by a five (leg 1 C18). geerts.com's heading is garbled ("seated by a five" -- likely "seated by a ..." OCR/typo for something else; the description mentions a sphere and an ouroboros tablet). Do not reproduce the title as given without verification.
- The Painter and his Model: date and originality (leg 2 F1; see C571). Essential Vermeer says c. 1690; Kok (2011) says 1700 / c. 1700. Worse: the Rijksmuseum panel is catalogued as a copy after Arnold ("Arnold van Houbraken (kopie naar)"), apparently after a Nicolaes Verkolje mezzotint after Arnold. The original's location is not established in this run. The study says "survives as a copy" and gives both dates.
- V&A dates the Schouburgh "1721-23" (leg 2 F2). Wrong on both ends: vols. I-III are 1718, 1719, 1721. The V&A page is otherwise excellent, but its date line must not be quoted.
- Jupiter, Juno and Io vs Juno, Apollo and Io (leg 2 F3). Geerts captions the picture "Juno, Apollo and Io" (53.3 x 50.8 cm); the reproduction trade calls it "Jupiter, Juno and Io". The third god's identity is unresolved in this run; the picture is kept in Beyond the 20, not in the 20.
- Stadel sitter and dimensions (leg 2 F4). The Stadel titles the portrait "Portrait of a Man (Rudolf van Loen?)" -- the sitter is a guess. Its object page gives no dimensions; 42.7 x 33.8 cm comes from the 1995 Stadel catalogue as cited by a dealer.
- Author-portrait size (leg 2 F7). Horn: plate 158 x 105 mm, Schouburgh III, 1721, before p. 1. NPG: paper 162 x 101 mm. Plate vs paper -- not a contradiction, but the two numbers must not be mixed.
- Het Amsterdamsche Kermis Feest is NOT Arnold's (leg 2 F8). It is an engraving by Jacobus Houbraken after Cornelis Troost (one of the 32 Troost scenes). The Invaluable Arnold page lists it under Arnold; excluded from the 20 and from Beyond the 20's Arnold list.
- No Houbraken Pygmalion found (leg 2 F9). The task asked to check "Pygmalion". Searches returned Goltzius (1593) and Crispijn de Passe (1602-04) Pygmalions, and Kok's discussion of the Pygmalion topos in connection with The Painter and his Model. No painting or print of Pygmalion by Arnold Houbraken was found in this run. Do not assert one exists.
- Two Psyche ceilings or one? (leg 2 F10). Geerts: Psyche taken to heaven and marries Amor, c. 350 x 900 cm, Dordrechts Museum, from a Groenmarkt house, in the museum since 1944. V&A: a ceiling panel Eros conducting Psyche into the Parnassus, "also housed in the Dordrecht Museum". Same ceiling under two titles, or two ceilings, is unresolved.
- Artist counts disagree (leg 2 F12). "Some 550 Dutch and Flemish artists" (auction catalogue, first edition) vs 1,207 artists named or discussed (RKD/Houbraken Translated). The 550 is biographies; the 1,207 is names mentioned anywhere in the text. Both are correct under different definitions.
- Boijmans holdings unconfirmed (leg 2 F13). EN Wikipedia lists Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen as a public collection for Arnold, but the Boijmans artist page returned only boilerplate (15 lines) and no Boijmans object was identified in this run. Do not name a Boijmans work.
