- Sacrifice of Iphigenia date (leg 2 F14). "1690-1700" (Rijksmuseum mirror), "1700" (several mirrors), "around 1690" (wahooart). The range is the safe form.
- Dido original vs copies (leg 2 F15). The signed original is Ringling SN273, "most likely depicted around 1700", "approximately the same" dimensions as the V&A copy (60.9 x 82 cm). The V&A copy is by an unidentified painter, monogram WM. A larger third version on canvas was sold at Sotheby's in 1978. None of the three versions has a museum-verified dimension for Arnold's own hand.
- geerts.com is an amateur site (leg 2 F16). It says so itself ("I am not an expert"). Its dimensions for W6, W12, W13, W19 and the Juno picture are reported, not verified, and it is the sole source for several of them.
- Forty-One Emblems undated (leg 2 F17). No publication date was found for the series; publisher Leonard Schenk. Do not date it.
- Sundial etching dimensions conflict in the mirror record (leg 2 F18). The Alamy/Rijksmuseum mirror gives both "h 71 mm x w 84 mm" and "h 158 mm x w 96 mm" for the same sheet (plate vs sheet, presumably). Neither was confirmed on a Rijksmuseum page.
- ADB's own error on Arnold (#545, leg 1 C8, carried). The ADB article gives Arnold Houbraken as born "1669" in Dordrecht; he was born 28 March 1660. The ADB is a 19th-century source and errs on the father's dates -- a warning against treating it as exact on the son's.

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

Of the 20 documented works (W1-W20): VERIFIED (dimensions from a museum or collection page read end to end in leg 2) -- 2: W16 Aeneas and the Sibyl in the Underworld (9.6 x 13.1 cm, Met); W17 Portrait of Arnold Houbraken (162 x 101 mm paper size, NPG). REPORTED (dimensions from a page read end to end, but a secondary source, dealer page or mirror of museum data) -- 14: W1 (71 x 84 mm, mirror of Rijksmuseum data), W4 (28.5 x 19 cm, Essential Vermeer), W5 (80 x 64 cm, mirrors of Rijksmuseum data; Horn gives 79.5 x 63.4 cm), W6 (66 x 79 cm, geerts), W7 (42.7 x 33.8 cm, 1995 catalogue via White Rose), W8 (c. 60.9 x 82 cm, V&A copy dimensions applied to the original), W9 (21.5 x 18 cm, Moes via White Rose), W10 (42.9 x 34.3 cm, White Rose dealer page), W11 (43 x 34.5 cm, White Rose dealer page), W12 (71 x 96 cm, geerts), W13 (41.3 x 49.5 cm, geerts), W15 (367 x 276 mm, mirror of Rijksmuseum data), W18 (page counts from auction catalogues), W19 (c. 350 x 900 cm, geerts). NOT VERIFIED -- 4: W2 (no dimensions found), W3 (no dimensions in the snippet read), W14 (sheets vary; no per-sheet museum dimensions), W20 (no dimensions or collation found). Note on the count: the V&A Dido page (read end to end, 138 lines) DOES give dimensions -- approx. 60.9 x 82 cm -- but for an early copy after Arnold, not for Arnold's signed original (W8). Those dimensions therefore cannot verify W8; they are reported for the copy only. Similarly the Stadel page (read end to end, 36 lines) gives no dimensions at all for W7, so W7 stays reported-but-unverified on the 1995 catalogue figure. (leg 2 Dimensions tally.)

## Aesthetic logic

Houbraken's aesthetic is a provincial classicism with a deist spine. Everything in his work -- the emblem dictionaries, the theatre of lives, the oval portrait plates, the small learned history panels -- is a machine for rescuing names from the dark night of death, and the machine runs on faces: if the face is convincing, the life is true. He is a compiler who knows he is a compiler, and says so: he uses the most recent source even when it is copied, admits incompleteness, corrects himself in public, and begs for help. The honesty about method is what separates him from a mere plagiarist and what makes the book still usable: its errors are traceable. His classicism is late, provincial and schoolmasterly -- and that is the point. He is not inventing theory, he is packaging it for young history painters in the provinces, in Dutch, with pictures. The Schouburgh is a teaching machine, not a treatise. The family is the firm: father draws, son engraves, daughters embellish, wife finishes the book after his death -- and the "dynasty" bias he shows in the book (extra space for founders of painter dynasties) is self-portraiture, since he considered himself the founder of his own dynasty. His omissions are the omissions of his sources, and his biases are the biases of his position: Vermeer mentioned once, Catholics under-represented, landscapists undervalued -- the shape of the network he could reach and the genre hierarchy he inherited, not personal failures. He is the hinge between the Golden Age and its history: too late to be a Golden Age painter and too early to be a modern art historian; his work is the moment a living tradition becomes a written record -- compiled in haste, by quill, in three years, while dying. That urgency is the book's real subject. (leg 1 P1-P10, condensed.)

## Positions

- P1. Deistic classicism. His classicism is not just taste but theology: a hands-off God whose plan is glimpsed only through nature makes the study of nature, antiquity and human character the only honest religion -- and history painting its liturgy. The biographies, the emblems and the Schouburgh are one project: recording the order of the world before death erases it.
- P2. Rescue from oblivion is the work. "The dark night of death" ends every life; the only counter-move is to record names and works for posterity. The title print (Art breaking Time's scythe) is the mission statement; the book is a lifeboat, not a monument.
- P3. Biography and theory are one volume because the market and the mission demand it. A book of pure theory would sell poorly and be a financial liability; biographies are what readers seek. So theory is smuggled inside lives (M5) -- a commercial decision that became a literary form.
- P4. The face is the unit of truth. From the 1687 anti-papal title print to the Rembrandt praise to the 43 portrait plates, Houbraken trusts the face more than the document. Physiognomy is his epistemology: if the face is convincing, the life is true.
- P5. He is a compiler who knows he is a compiler -- and says so. He uses the most recent source even when it is copied, admits incompleteness, corrects himself in public, and begs for help. The honesty about method is what separates him from a mere plagiarist and what makes the book still usable: its errors are traceable.
- P6. His classicism is a late, provincial, schoolmasterly classicism -- and that is the point. Emmens' insult ("compilatory, thoughtless and narrowminded") misreads the project: Houbraken is not inventing theory, he is packaging it for young history painters in the provinces, in Dutch, with pictures. The Schouburgh is a teaching machine, not a treatise.
- P7. The family is the firm. The Schouburgh is a household production in the literal sense: father draws, son engraves, daughters embellish, wife finishes the book after his death. The "dynasty" bias he shows in the book (extra space for founders of painter dynasties) is self-portraiture -- he considered himself the founder of his own dynasty.
- P8. Flattery is a survival skill, not a vice. His recurring theme -- artists ruined because they failed to flatter a patron -- is autobiography: the Fagel dedication refused, Schuylenburch's "slight smile and scant thanks", the subscription price set too low. The book's moral economy is the economy of a man who lived it.
- P9. His omissions are the omissions of his sources, and his biases are the biases of his position. Vermeer mentioned once, Catholics under-represented, landscapists undervalued -- these are not personal failures so much as the shape of the network he could reach (Guild contacts, Dordrecht antiquarians, van der Vinne's list) and the genre hierarchy he inherited. Judging him by today's canon mistakes the map for the territory.
- P10. He is the hinge between the Golden Age and its history. He is too late to be a Golden Age painter and too early to be a modern art historian; his work is the moment a living tradition becomes a written record -- compiled in haste, by quill, in three years, while dying. That urgency is the book's real subject.

## Quotes

- Q1. "The foundation of everything I know about art" -- Houbraken on his years with Samuel van Hoogstraten (Groote schouburgh I, 1718, p. 155; quoted in Horn 2023, p. 38).
- Q2. "Well done is much done." -- Houbraken's wisdom, one of the two mottos of the RKD Houbraken Translated project (Foreword).
- Q3. "To be helpful to each other's benefit (in any form whatsoever) as well as to one's own is the chain that links human society." -- Houbraken, second motto of Houbraken Translated (Foreword).
- Q4. "His art fortune offered him a pleasing and friendly face now and then, but when he expected to embrace her, she slipped like water through his fingers." -- Johan van Gool (1750, p. 146) on Houbraken's career, quoted in Horn 2023, p. 212.
- Q5. "Our honest artist always shed behind the net, ploughed the parched beach and hunted where there was naught to be caught." -- Johan van Gool (1750, p. 138) on the Schouburgh's finances, quoted in Horn 2023, p. 219.
- Q6. "The goddess of Art transported by diligence represents the passion for art of the writer who spared himself neither effort nor application to bring to light anew the name and fame of artists who had been obscured by time... the portraits pitched upside down, quietly secured from the gnawing of worms, so that youthful artists may decorate them with palm and greening bay leaves, or carry them to eternity." -- anonymous review poem, April 1718, on the vol. I title print (in Houbraken Translated; quoted in Horn 2023, pp. 212-213).
- Q7. "Had a few more years passed, one would hardly have been able to unearth anything about many of these artists who now, because of the author's tireless diligence and restless enquiries, have been saved from oblivion and will therefore live forever." -- anonymous review, Maendelyke uittreksels, April 1718 (published by Cornelis 1998, p. 152; quoted in Horn 2023, p. 214).
- Q8. On Rembrandt's self-portrait in Jan van Beuningen's collection: "so artfully and vigorously executed that the most powerful brushwork of Van Dyck and Rubens could not rival it; indeed the head seemed to issue from the piece and speak to the beholders." -- Houbraken, Groote schouburgh I, 1718, p. 269 (quoted in Slive 1953, ch. XI, n.2; translation Slive's).
- Q9. On Rembrandt's invention: "He was rich in thoughts as regards art, so that one not seldom sees a multitude of different sketches of one and the same subject by him, full of changes in faces, poses and costumes, in which he is to be praised above others -- especially those who use the same faces and costumes in their works as if they were all twins. Indeed he excelled all in this, and I know nobody who has made so many changes in sketches of one and the same subject." -- Houbraken, Groote schouburgh I, 1718, pp. 257-258 (quoted in Slive 1953, ch. XI, n.2).
- Q10. "A competent if rather uninspired academic painter, but his De Groote Schouburgh is the single most important source of information about the great Dutch painters of the 17th century." -- Encyclopaedia Britannica on Arnold Houbraken.
- Q11. "A history painter of considerable finesse and erudition, whose treatment of rarified subjects is especially noteworthy." -- Alan Chong on Houbraken, quoted in Horn 2023, p. 17.
- Q12. "Houbraken's classicism is a 'compilatory, thoughtless and narrowminded' version of the ideas of Gerard de Lairesse and no more than an 'extremely provincial and schoolmasterish version of the French'." -- Jan Emmens (1968 [1964], pp. 102-103), quoted in Horn 2023, p. 214. (Hostile; include as the counter-position, not as fact.)
- Q13. "No engraver has ever equaled, and probably will not equal, the Dutchman Jacobus Houbraken, in the manner of imitating the flesh and the hair by means of the graver." -- Raphael Morghen on his son Jacobus (geerts.com, quoting the elder tradition; about Jacobus, not Arnold -- included for the family-workshop context, flagged as such).
- Q14. On Gelsdorf (George Geldorp): "Sandrart mentions also at that time an Englishman named GELSDORF, who made many portraits in England. But he could not draw, so he was forced to have someone else draw the outline of the face on paper, which he then pricked through with a needle and pounced onto his panel. We meet such a sort now and then more often. But we have not cut our pen for crutches and broken legs." -- Houbraken, Groote schouburgh II, 1719, p. 88 (quoted in EN Wikipedia "Schouburgh", Notes). Shows his contempt for painters who cannot draw -- and his own standard.

## Network

Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 Dordrecht - 14 October 1719 Amsterdam). EN Wikipedia read end to end in legs 1, 2 and 3 (171 lines each). 57 unique persons in the direct network roster below (leg 3 counted 57: teachers 4, family 4, pupils 2, collaborators 3, publishers 6, sources 7, poets/dedicatees 4, subjects 9, circle 18). Not counted in the 57: Arnold himself; the 7 unnamed children; second-ring names (Jan Meyssens, de Bie's publisher; Frans van Hoogstraten, David's father; Brouerius van Nidek; Rembrandt and Frans Hals as already-carried subjects).

Teachers (4): Johannes de Haan -- threadtwisting (twyndraat) master, Arnold's first teacher for two years; introduced him to engraving. No dates found. Willem van Drielenburch (1632 Utrecht - c. 1687 Dordrecht) -- Arnold's art teacher during the rampjaar 1672; pupil of Abraham Bloemaert (studied #531); landscape in the manner of Jan Both (studied #533); came to Dordrecht 1668; "Houbraken didn't learn much that year, because the unrest in the city was such that Drielenburg was constantly sending him out to hear the latest news" (EN Wikipedia). Also teacher of Wilhelmus Beurs. FLAG: spelling Drielenburch / Drielenburg / Drillenburg. Jacob Levecq (c. 1634-1675) -- Rembrandt pupil 1653-1655; Arnold his pupil for the last nine months of his life; on Levecq's death Arnold inherited a third of his prints, choosing Lucas van Leyden and Durer. Already carried (#545 spare 6). Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-1678) -- Arnold's teacher for four years; Rembrandt pupil; author of Inleyding tot de hooge schoole der schilderkonst (1678), which Houbraken referenced. Already carried (#545 spare 5).

Family (4 named; 7 further children unnamed): Sara Sasbout -- married Arnold 1685; published vol. 3 of the Schouburgh posthumously (1721) with the children; imprint "Gedrukt voor de Weduwe des Autheurs". No dates found. Jacobus Houbraken (1698-1780) -- son; studied #545. Engraved the Schouburgh portraits; assisted his mother with the last proofs. Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736) -- daughter; draughtswoman; topographical drawings for the Schoemaker albums; married Jacobus Stellingwerff 1723; assisted father and brother on the Schouburgh designs; designs for Pieter Tanje. See spares. Christina Houbraken (c. 1695-1760s) -- daughter; married the painter Anthony Elliger 1724; mother of the pastel artist Christina Maria Elliger; no works survive. See extra spares.

Pupils (3; Jacobus counted under family): Matthijs Balen (1684-1766) -- registered pupil; appears in van Gool's Nieuwe Schouburg Part II, p. 55; born and died in Dordrecht; lived in The Hague 1705-1715; Italianate landscapes, portraits and historical allegories (dates parent-verified in this run, see C568). Johan Graham -- registered pupil; appears in van Gool's Nieuwe Schouburg Part II, p. 276. No dates found (leg 3 S13).

Collaborators -- the picture bible and the book (3): Gerard Hoet (1648-1733) -- designed 119 of the 214 picture-bible (Taferelen) designs; Arnold designed 27 beside Hoet's 119 and Picart's 64. Already carried (#544 main 2). FLAG (carried from #545 log): 119 + 27 + 64 = 210, not 214 -- arithmetic gap unresolved (see C575). Bernard Picart (1673-1733) -- 64 picture-bible designs; studied #544. Gilliam van der Gouwen (c. 1657 - buried 15 March 1716) -- engraved 41 of the 214 picture-bible plates. Already carried (#544 extra spares).

Publishers of the Schouburgh (6): Arnold self-published vols. 1-2 (1718, 1719, "Gedrukt voor den autheur"); widow Sara published vol. 3 (1721, "Gedrukt voor de Weduwe des Autheurs"). Johannes Swart (1685-1759), Cornelis Boucquet (1696-1777), Mattheus Gaillard (1707-1766) -- The Hague booksellers/auctioneers who published the second edition 1753; "the moving force behind the publication was Houbraken's good friend Johan van Gool" (RKD, Houbraken Translated). Dates from the RKD page (leg 3 S1). Piet Swillens (1890-1963) -- modern edition 1943/1944/1953 (reformatted, with index and survey of Houbraken's sources). Alfred von Wurzbach -- German translation, Vienna 1880 (Quellenschrift fur Kunstgeschichte vol. XIV); omitted passages he considered unimportant. 1976 photo-engraved reprint by B.M. Israel, Amsterdam.

Models / sources -- the writers Arnold built on (7): Karel van Mander (1548-1606) -- Schilder-boeck (1604); the Schouburgh is explicitly "een vervolg op het Schilderboek van K. v. Mander", vol. 1 "simply meant as an addendum" listing ~200 artists born 1466-1613 omitted by van Mander. ALREADY CARRIED -- not proposed as new. FLAG: death 2 September 1606 (EN Wikipedia, Britannica) vs 11 September 1606 (Stadel, GAMEO, Wikimedia Commons). Joachim von Sandrart (12 May 1606 - 14 October 1688) -- Teutsche Academie (1675-1680), inspired by van Mander, Italian sections after Vasari; active in Amsterdam c. 1637-1645; Honthorst pupil. ALREADY CARRIED -- not proposed as new. FLAG: birth 12 May 1606 (Wikipedia, HMdb marker) vs 21 May 1606 (sandrart.net). Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715) -- Het Gulden Cabinet (Antwerp, 1662), commissioned by the publisher Jan Meyssens as the South Netherlandish counterpart to van Mander; "formed the principal source of information for later art historians such as Arnold Houbraken and Jacob Campo Weyerman" (EN Wikipedia, Het Gulden Cabinet article). See spares. FLAG: death c. 1712-1715 (Wikipedia) vs 1715 (Commons) vs "after 1711" (Swann, "1627-1711"); publication 1661 (title-page plate) vs 1662 (colophon). Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574) -- Lives (1550; enlarged illustrated 1568); the genre's inventor; van Mander's and Sandrart's model. See spares. Roger de Piles (7 October 1635 - 5 April 1709) -- Dialogue sur le coloris (1673), Abrege de la vie des peintres (1699, 7 vols. of lives), Cours de peinture par principes avec un balance de peintres (1708): 56 painters scored on composition, drawing, colour and expression. Portrait by Bernard Picart, 1704. See extra spares. FLAG: score scale 0-18 (Wikipedia) vs 20-point scale (Graddy, Brandeis working paper). Filippo Baldinucci (1625-1696) -- Notizie de' professori del disegno (1681-1728, 6 vols., last three by his son Francesco Saverio); first history of engraving and first dictionary of artistic terms (both 1686); first to make full use of documents; corrects and continues Vasari. See spares. FLAG: death 1696, NOT 1697 -- "1625-1697" as commonly cited is wrong (Uffizi: Florence 1625 - Florence 1696; Britannica: died 1 Jan 1696; CalendarZ: 3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696); birth c. 1624 (Britannica, Columbia) vs 1625 (Uffizi, CalendarZ). Vincent van der Vinne (d. 1702) -- Houbraken used a competency list drawn up by van der Vinne before his death in 1702, and "went to great pains to add entries for the entire list of painters mentioned in a poem about the Bentvueghels". (Vincent van der Vinne the Younger? The Schouburgh article does not give dates; not proposed -- the identification is not settled in this run.)

Poets and dedicatees (4): David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724) -- physician, poet, philologist; 30-line laudatory poem in Schouburgh vol. 1 (1718); prorector of the Amsterdam Latin School; Phaedrus edition 1701 (his masterpiece); Groot woordenboek 1725-1733 (completed by van Nidek). See spares. FLAG: he is Samuel van Hoogstraten's NEPHEW (son of Samuel's brother Frans), not his son -- Weststeijn, The Visible World. Jakob Zeeus -- poet; Arnold's 1711 poem "Aen den heere Jakob Zeeus, den Wolf in 't schaepsvel ter drukpersse bestellende" is addressed to him. No dates found (leg 3 S16 returned only the Gysbert Japicx dictionary entry). Johan van Schuylenburch (1675-1735) -- dedicatee of vol. 1 (two-page dedication, 1718). Dates from the RKD page only (leg 3 S1). See extra spares. Pieter de la Court van der Voort (1664-1739) -- dedicatee of vol. 2 (1719); Leiden cloth merchant and collector. Dates from the RKD page only (leg 3 S1). See extra spares.

Subjects -- painters Arnold wrote about (9; Rembrandt, Hals and the rest of the carried chain are not re-counted here): Aert de Gelder (1645-1727), Nicolaes Maes (1634-1693), Godfried Schalcken (1643-1706), Adriaen van der Werff (1659-1722), Frans van Mieris the Elder (1635-1681), Caspar Netscher (c. 1639-1684), Eglon van der Neer (1635/36-1703), Arnold Boonen (1669-1729), Gerard de Lairesse (1640-1711; already carried, held back from #528). All nine except Lairesse are proposed below. Houbraken is the cited source for: de Gelder's move to Rembrandt ("to learn how to paint in Rembrandt's manner", Leiden Collection); Mieris's birth date ("born, according to Houbraken, at Leiden on the 16th of April 1635", 1911 Britannica); Netscher's birth and death ("According to Houbraken...", Leiden Collection, twice); Eglon van der Neer's age at death ("died in 1703 at age seventy", Invaluable) and his single pupil ("Houbraken mentions only one pupil, Adriaen van der Werff", Invaluable); Schalcken's Lady, Come into the Garden ("singled out by Schalcken's pupil and Dutch artistic biographer Arnold Houbraken as representative of his oeuvre", EN Wikipedia); van der Werff's standing ("Arnold Houbraken, writing in 1718, considered him the greatest of the Dutch painters", EN Wikipedia).

Amsterdam / Dordrecht circles -- further named persons (18): Jan van Gool (1685-1763) -- "his friend Arnold Houbraken"; Nieuwe Schouburg (1750) meant as an update; drove the 1753 reprint. Already carried (#545 main 3); EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3 (89 lines). His Nieuwe Schouburg painter lists name, among others: Arnold Houbraken (I, p. 131), Frans van Mieris the Elder (II, p. 147), Adriaen van der Werff (II, p. 376), Gerard Hoet (II, p. 415), Arnold Boonen (I, p. 294), Matthijs Balen (II, p. 55), Johan Graham (II, p. 276), Jacob Campo Weyerman (I, p. 434), Aert Schouman (II, p. 346). Adriaan van der Willigen (1766-1841, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- first modern update of Houbraken, 1866. Already carried (#545 extra spares). Jacobus Stellingwerff (d. December 1727) -- draughtsman; Antonina's husband (m. May 1723); topographical drawings for Brouerius van Nidek and the Schoemaker albums. Andries and Gerrit Schoemaker -- collectors of topographical prints and drawings; Antonina made a large portion of their album drawings. Anthony Elliger -- painter; Christina's husband (m. 1724); father of Christina Maria Elliger, pastel artist. Pieter Tanje -- engraver; Antonina provided designs for his print projects. Cornelis Troost -- Boonen's most famous pupil (actor turned painter; already carried as a name inside the #541/#542 chain -- "teacher of Buys and Tanje; pupil of Arnold Boonen" is a carried entry about Troost himself, so Troost is NOT proposed here). Philip van Dijk -- van der Werff's pupil (among others); also a pupil of Arnold Boonen per some sources -- not pursued. Wilhelmus Beurs -- Drielenburch's pupil (RKD). Maria Schalcken (c. 1645/49 - before 1699) -- Godfried's sister and pupil, painter in the same candlelight manner. Justus de Gelder, Margaretha van Godewijk, Jacob Moelaert, Johannes Vollevens -- Maes's registered pupils in Dordrecht. ## Future candidates
Top pick for #547 is Aert de Gelder (26 October 1645 - 27 August 1727) - the Dordrecht townsman who learned from Arnold's teacher and carried Rembrandt's late manner into the eighteenth century. First trained under Samuel van Hoogstraten in Dordrecht (fellow pupil with Schalcken), then Rembrandt's last pupil in Amsterdam; back in Dordrecht after 1669 for the rest of his life; the only Dutch artist to paint in Rembrandt's late style into the 18th century -- impasto laid on with daubs and scratches, candlelit chiaroscuro, the 22-painting Passion series (c. 1715), and Jacob's Dream, long attributed to Rembrandt himself. Houbraken is the primary source for his life ("According to Houbraken, De Gelder headed to Amsterdam 'to learn how to paint in Rembrandt's manner'"). He is the top pick because he wins on both tests: the direct link to Arnold (two independent strands -- Houbraken-as-biographer plus shared master plus shared town, stated as that, not as a proven friendship; no document found in this run proves the two men met) and the strongest visual mechanism among the new names (the late Rembrandt manner carried into the eighteenth century, the impasto, the serial Passion, the attribution problem as a built-in test). Against the carried mains, stated plainly: Jan van Gool (carried main 3) is the closest TEXTUAL heir -- the sequel-writer, "his friend Arnold Houbraken", driver of the 1753 reprint -- and the chain logic of the series favours him; if the #547 run prefers the book-line, van Gool is the pick. But his mechanism repeats Arnold's own (another book of lives), while de Gelder opens a new visual line. George Vertue and Isaak Tirion (carried mains 1-2) are Jacobus's London and Amsterdam trade, not Arnold's; their links to Arnold run through the son. The doorway is the Dordrecht townsman who learned from Arnold's teacher and carried Rembrandt's late manner into the eighteenth century -- the life Houbraken himself wrote. Adriaen van der Werff (21 January 1659 - 12 November 1722; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 126 lines) - main 2: the polished opposite of de Gelder, and the painter Arnold himself ranked highest: "Arnold Houbraken, writing in 1718, considered him the greatest of the Dutch painters". Pupil of Eglon van der Neer (1671-1676); Rotterdam master at 17; head of the Rotterdam Guild of St Luke 1691 and 1695; court painter to Johann Wilhelm, Elector Palatine, from 1696/97, knighted 1703; his enamel-smooth "porcelain finish" carried the Leiden fijnschilder manner onto large history pictures for the Dusseldorf court, his 34 paintings hung in a room next to Rembrandt's; reputation collapsed in the 19th century as a betrayer of the Dutch naturalistic tradition. He is main 2 rather than top pick because his link to Arnold is a verdict, not a shared workshop: Arnold crowned him, where de Gelder is Arnold's teacher-brother and townsman. The doorway is the painter Arnold called the greatest of the Dutch painters -- the porcelain finish against de Gelder's impasto. FLAG: EN Wikipedia says both "In December 1704, he painted Prince John Churchill" and lists the Marlborough portrait as "(12 December 1701)"; "His only daughter married to Flemish painter Adriaen Brouwer" is impossible (Brouwer died 1638) and is not used here; Eglon tutelage 1671-1676 (Werff article) vs 1671-1675 (Leiden Collection). Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693, buried 24 December 1693; dates search-verified only) - main 3: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who broke away and invented the eavesdropper. In Rembrandt's Amsterdam studio c. 1648-1653, back in Dordrecht by December 1653 for twenty years, then Amsterdam from 1673, where he painted some 900 portraits and died rich. His Dordrecht genre pictures (1655-1665) are the hinge of the chain: the eavesdropper at the foot of the stairs, finger to her lips, breaking the fourth wall -- a domestic interior as a listening machine, the exact subject Vermeer would inherit. His registered Dordrecht pupils included Justus de Gelder, Margaretha van Godewijk, Jacob Moelaert and Johannes Vollevens. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he left Dordrecht for Amsterdam portraiture, where de Gelder stayed and kept Rembrandt's manner alive in the town itself. The doorway is the Dordrecht pupil who left Rembrandt, invented the eavesdropper, and then painted nine hundred portraits. FLAG: death 24 December 1693 (burial, Wikipedia) vs 24 November 1693 (Prabook, Alchetron). Godfried Schalcken (8 October 1643 - 16 November 1706; dates search-verified only) - the reserve: the candlelight painter. Hoogstraten pupil in Dordrecht from 1656 (fellow pupil with de Gelder), then Gerard Dou in Leiden c. 1663; back in Dordrecht until 1691, then The Hague; the first fijnschilder from outside Leiden, and the one who made a whole oeuvre out of a single light source -- the kaarslichtjes, figures in nocturnal settings lit by candle, lamp or lantern, with diffuse candlelight softening the sitter's face (his candlelit William III; his nocturnal self-portrait sent to Cosimo III). Arnold singled out his Lady, Come into the Garden as representative of his oeuvre, and Schalcken taught Arnold Boonen. He is the reserve because his mechanism (one light source, endlessly varied) is narrower than the mains', though it is the most directly generative of all the Dordrecht mechanisms. The doorway is the candlelight painter whose one light source Arnold singled out. FLAG: born Made (EN Wikipedia, Leiden Collection) vs "born at Dordrecht" (Bryan's, WahooArt). Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681; dates search-verified only) - alternate 1: the Leiden fine painter at his smallest and most polished. Goldsmith's son; glass-painter's pupil, then Gerard Dou's "Prince of my students"; panels seldom larger than 12-15 inches; shiny brightness and metallic polish, satin skirts, oyster luncheons and doctor's visits; founder of a three-generation Leiden dynasty (Willem, Frans the Younger). Houbraken is the source for his birth date, and the 1911 Britannica already doubts it. He is alternate 1 because his mechanism (the miniature polish) overlaps van der Werff's, and his life is short on incident -- but he is the fijnschilder standard against which Werff and Schalcken are measured. The doorway is the prince of Dou's students, painting satin on panels the size of a hand. FLAG: birth 16 April 1635 "according to Houbraken" (1911 Britannica: "It is a question whether Houbraken has truly recorded this master's birthday" -- an oyster-luncheon picture is dated 1650); "? 1635" in the 1911 Britannica heading. Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684; dates search-verified only) - alternate 2: the Hague portraitist who painted silk. Orphaned young, raised in Arnhem, ter Borch's most gifted pupil in Deventer (c. 1655-1659), then The Hague from 1662, where from the 1670s he was the most sought-after portrait painter in the city; master of oriental rugs, silk and brocade, "introduced an international style to the Northern Netherlands"; painted lying in bed with gout until 15 January 1684, when, "according to Houbraken, 'this affliction affected his bowls, and caused his death'". He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (fabric as the picture) is a single brilliant trick rather than a system -- but it is the trick the whole Hague portrait trade ran on. The doorway is the painter who sold silk by the yard and died painting in bed. Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729; dates search-verified only) - spare 1: the Dordrecht portraitist who carried the candle out of the genre piece and into the state portrait. Pupil first of Arnold Verbuis, then six years (from 1683) under Schalcken; small candlelit genre pictures before 1700, then life-size portraits at the German courts (1694-95) and in Amsterdam from 1696 -- Peter the Great, Marlborough, the Prince and Princess of Orange; teacher of Cornelis Troost. The doorway is the Schalcken pupil who took candlelight to the German courts and came home to paint Peter the Great. Eglon Hendrik van der Neer (1635/36 - 3 May 1703; dates search-verified only) - spare 2: the teacher between the generations: son of the moonlit landscape painter Aert van der Neer, pupil of Jacob van Loo, friend of Frans van Mieris the Elder (probably known since 1658), and the man who taught Adriaen van der Werff in Rotterdam (1671-1675/76) -- "Houbraken mentions only one pupil, Adriaen van der Werff". Court painter to Charles II of Spain (1687) and to the Elector Palatine in Dusseldorf (1690/1698); married the artist Adriana Spilberg 1697; died in Dusseldorf 1703. Elegant fashionable people, then landscapes; the figure-painter who put the figures into Jan van der Heyden's town views. The doorway is the teacher of the greatest Dutch painter -- the link between Mieris and van der Werff. Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736, buried 12 December 1736; dates search-verified only) - spare 3: PROMOTED from the #545 extra verified spares, where she was held back only because no dates were established; this run establishes them (born 30 May 1686, baptised 31 May 1686 -- see C569). The daughter who drew the country. Draughtswoman and portraitist; learned in her father's workshop; assisted father and brother on the Schouburgh designs and provided designs for Pieter Tanje; married the draughtsman Jacobus Stellingwerff 1723 and, after his death in 1727, kept supplying topographical drawings to the collectors Andries and Gerrit Schoemaker until at least 1729 -- hundreds of Dutch sites, towns and buildings drawn on the spot, later embellished with animals and people. The doorway is the daughter who drew the whole country for the collectors' albums. David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724; dates search-verified only) - spare 4: the poet inside the painter's book. Physician, poet, philologist; Samuel van Hoogstraten's nephew (son of Samuel's brother Frans); prorector of the Amsterdam Latin School; his annotated Latin Phaedrus edition (1701) is his masterpiece, his Dutch verse Phaedrus (1703) a bestseller; his 30-line laudatory poem opens Schouburgh vol. 1; his Groot woordenboek (1725-1733, completed by van Nidek) is the ten-volume dictionary of the Republic of Letters; 61 wedding poems make him one of the most productive occasional poets of the age. The doorway is the poet whose thirty lines open Arnold's book. Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574; dates search-verified only) - spare 5: the root of the whole genre: painter, architect (the Uffizi, begun 1560; the corridor, 1565) and the inventor of the encyclopedia of artistic biographies -- Le Vite (1550; partly rewritten, enlarged and given woodcut portraits, some imaginary, in 1568), dedicated to Cosimo I de' Medici, with its treatise on technique and its Florentine bias; van Mander and Sandrart both built their Italian sections on him, and Houbraken's book is the third generation of his invention. He is a spare, not a main, because his link to Arnold runs through van Mander and Sandrart -- two removes -- and his mechanism (canon-making by biography) is the series' background, not its next step. The doorway is the man who invented the book Arnold spent his life continuing. Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715; dates search-verified only) - spare 6: the Antwerp rhetorician who wrote the South's answer to van Mander. Het Gulden Cabinet (Antwerp, 1662), commissioned by the publisher Jan Meyssens as "the South Netherlandish counterpart of Karel van Mander's Schilder-Boeck": three parts, 95 full-page engraved portraits, biographies and panegyrics of 16th- and 17th-century artists, dedicated to the collector Antoon van Leyen; "the principal source of information for later art historians such as Arnold Houbraken and Jacob Campo Weyerman". A notary and rederijker who deemed it his duty to broadcast the fame of the artists -- the panegyric as a system, against Houbraken's anecdotal biography. The doorway is the Antwerp rhetorician whose Golden Cabinet Houbraken mined. Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696; dates search-verified only) - spare 7: the Florentine who corrected Vasari with documents. Cardinal Leopoldo de' Medici's man, put in charge of the Medici drawing collection (the nucleus of the Uffizi's); Notizie de' professori del disegno (1681-1728, six volumes, the last three finished by his son Francesco Saverio), the first universal history of the figurative arts in Europe and "the first to make full use of documents"; the first history of engraving and the first dictionary of artistic terms (both 1686); dozens of Flemish and Dutch painters, including Rembrandt, judged worthy of inclusion. The doorway is the archivist who corrected Vasari with documents -- and wrote the first history of engraving. Willem van Drielenburch (1632 Utrecht - c. 1687 Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) - spare 8: the rampjaar teacher. Bloemaert pupil, landscape painter in the manner of Jan Both; came to Dordrecht 1668; Arnold his pupil in 1672, the year of disaster, when the boy learned less painting than news -- his master "was constantly sending him out to hear the latest news". Also teacher of Wilhelmus Beurs. A small oeuvre and a thinner record than the other spares, but the anecdote is the mechanism: the pupil as messenger, the studio as a newsroom, the year the country nearly ended. The doorway is the teacher who sent his pupil out for the news in the year of disaster. (The 14 names carried in #545 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756), Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam), Jan van Gool (1685-1763) (mains); Jan Wagenaar (25 October 1709 - 1 March 1773) (reserve); Thomas Birch (1705-1766), Gerard Edelinck (bapt. 20 October 1640 - 2 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Cornelis Cort (c.1533-1578), Jonas Suyderhoef (1613-1686), Claes Jansz. Visscher (1587 - 19 June 1652), Nicolaes Visscher I (25 January 1618 - buried 11 September 1679), Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-1678), Jacob Levecq (c.1634-1675), Aert Schouman (4 March 1710 - 5 July 1792), Bartholomeus van der Helst (1613 - buried 16 December 1670). George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 123 lines) - the London engraver Houbraken superseded on the *Heads*. Apprenticed at 13, seven years under Michael Vandergucht, pupil of Thomas Gibson at Kneller's academy; Rose and Crown Club with Hogarth and Tillemans; official engraver to the Society of Antiquaries 1717, the only engraver made Fellow; ~500 portraits + ~500 antiquarian plates; *Twelve Heads of Poets* 1730; Rapin's *History of England* for the Knaptons 1736; his *Heads* contribution "submerged by the popularity of Houbraken's 'brilliant but less truthful productions'"; his forty volumes of notebooks, bought by Walpole, became *Anecdotes of Painting in England*. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the man Houbraken displaced, not the man Houbraken came from: the London half of the portrait trade, where Arnold is the Amsterdam root. The doorway is the London engraver whose plates Houbraken's brighter ones displaced. The parent confirms the promotion (see C561). Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 21 lines) - the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. *Hedendaagsche historie* and *Vaderlandse Historie*, "illustrated with engravings by leading artists of Amsterdam"; moved to Amsterdam by May 1727; 1742 bought Wetstein's Kalverstraat house; *Het Verheerlykt Nederland* 1745-57, continued by his widow 1773-74. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who commissioned the faces, where Arnold is the man who wrote the book that needed them. The doorway is the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. Jan van Gool (1685-1763; dates search-verified only) - the Hague painter-biographer whose sequel Houbraken illustrated. *Nieuwe Schouburg* (The Hague, 1750), "meant as an update to the original" Schouburgh; regent then director of the Hague Drawing School 1720-1734; Italianate landscapes; his book carries notes on the Hague painters and the founding of the Hague drawing academy. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the sequel, not the source: the man who continued Arnold's book, where Arnold is the man who wrote it. The doorway is the sequel-writer whose new Schouburgh Houbraken illustrated. Jan Wagenaar (25 October 1709 - 1 March 1773; dates search-verified only) - the reserve: the historian whose 21-part national history Houbraken illustrated for Tirion. *Vaderlandsche historie* (1749-1760, 21 vols), continued to 52 vols; also *Tegenwoordige staat* and the Amsterdam history (1760, 3 vols); at first refused to publish his name, revealing himself in vol. 20 (1759). He is the reserve because he is the text, not the hand: the man who wrote the history Houbraken pictured, but whose own drawn work is nil. Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 39 lines) - the pupil who carried Picart's book-illustration trade into English history publishing. Huguenot engraver, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the pupil who left the workshop for London, not the collaborator who stayed in it: the trade carried outward, where Houbraken is the line carried forward. The doorway is the pupil who engraved Vivaldi and then illustrated the history of England. Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 185 lines) - the painter-engraver who designed the Bible Picart's shop engraved. 119 of the 214 picture-bible designs are his -- more than Picart's own 64; founded a drawing academy at Utrecht 1697 with Hendrick Schoock; published a book on drawing 1712 (103 prints by Pieter Bodart); source for Houbraken's Bentvueghels list; art dealer with Jacques Ignatius de Roore; trained by his father Moses Hoet and Warnard van Rijsen. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw, where Houbraken is the man who engraved the faces. The doorway is the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the root before the trunk, not the continuation after it: the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave, where Houbraken is the son who carried the Amsterdam line on. The doorway is the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave. (The 11 names carried in #543 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819), Hermanus Numan (1744-1820), Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783), Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) (mains); Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809) (reserve); Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779), Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808) (alternates); spares: Pieter Barbiers II (1749-1842), Jacob Haafner (1754-1809), Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796), Jan Bulthuis (1750-1801). Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; the 20 "Andriessen" log mentions are all Louis Andriessen (1939-2021), the composer, studied in the Rzewski chain -- never Jurriaan; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates search-verified only (Alamy/WGA captions). Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the namesake met in Paris, a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Painter; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770. He is the reserve because he is a near-name collision with Jacob de Wit (study #541) and almost nothing is recorded beyond the Paris meeting -- but the meeting itself is dated and the confusion is worth one study to clear. The doorway is the namesake met in Paris -- a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight, a fellow of the Pax circle. Marine painter and etcher; fellow member of Pax Artium Nutrix with Vinkeles; at the club he "became friendly with prominent collectors such as Jacob de Vos and Cornelis Ploos van Amstel". He is alternate 1 because his life is short and his oeuvre small, but his Pax membership and his marine etchings put him at the centre of the circle Vinkeles drank in. The doorway is the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight -- a fellow of the Pax circle. He is alternate 2 because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man whose drawing of a room became Vinkeles's largest sheet. (The 13 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. Simon Fokke (1712-1784) - the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Pupil of J. C. Philips (proposed spare 1); the booksellers' engraver of small portraits and vignettes; plates for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche Historie 1749-59; Danish portraits for Tycho Hofman (1741); after Vernet, Avercamp, Terborch, Ribera and Troost (a burlesque Death of Dido); the title vignette for Rousseau's Discourse on Inequality (1755); his portrait was painted by John Greenwood after Jacobus Buys. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Jacobus Buys (19 November 1724 - 7 April 1801; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Painter and engraver; studied under Cornelis Pronk, Jacob de Wit and Cornelis Troost; director of the Amsterdam Drawing Academy; joined the Guild of St Luke in 1750; bas-reliefs, tapestry designs, book illustrations and theatre scenes. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the pupil line after the engraver: the man who learned the witje, the flower piece and the theatre scene from three different masters and turned them into a teaching post. The doorway is the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Tako Hajo Jelgersma (29 October 1702 - 24 March 1795; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years, and outlived him by forty-one. Painter; de Wit's follower in grisaille; pupil of Frans Decker and Wigerus Vitringa; moved to Haarlem in 1752; pupils Johan Bernard Brandhoff, Johannes Petrus van Horstok, Cornelis van Noorde, Martinus van der Jagt and Johannes Swertner; lived to 92. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the influence line itself, narrow and documented: the only named man who kept painting grisailles in de Wit's manner after de Wit was gone, and the proof that the witje outlived its inventor. The doorway is the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years -- and outlived him by forty-one. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#541 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Cornelis Troost (8 October 1696 - 7 March 1750; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the actor who became Amsterdam's other great painter, and taught de Wit's own pupils. Amsterdam's other leading 18th-century painter; teacher of Buys and Tanje; pupil of Arnold Boonen; actor turned painter; the NELRI series (Mauritshuis, 1739-40), the "Dutch Hogarth". He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the parallel line, not the continuation: the man who shared Amsterdam with de Wit, taught de Wit's pupils, and painted the comic theatre where de Wit painted the gods. The doorway is the actor who became Amsterdam's other great painter, and taught de Wit's own pupils. Dionys van Nijmegen (1705-1798; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Rotterdam pupil who took de Wit's ceilings to the Maas. Rotterdam wall and ceiling decorator; pupil of his father Elias van Nijmegen and of de Wit; joined the Rotterdam Guild of St Luke in 1743; portraits and wall and ceiling decorations; father and teacher of Gerard van Nijmegen. He is the reserve because he is the provincial echo of the Amsterdam system -- the same ceilings, overdoors and grisailles, one city down the river -- but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the least documented in this block. The doorway is the Rotterdam pupil who took de Wit's ceilings to the Maas. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Burial: Wikipedia gives 12 November 1708 at Westerkerk; BADA gives buried 17 November 1708. Death 7 November 1708 is consistent across sources. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. These look like two Molenaers conflated under one name; do not mix them. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. The doorway is the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29).
